SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

RATS, FLEAS, AND PLAGUE.

Investigations of the- plague in India con firm the idea that it may pass from the rat to man through the flea and other blood-sucking parasites. It has bcen proved that the rat flea (Pules clieol)i4) will attack man even when plenty of rats are present. If it has previously bitten an infected rat it may con- vey the plague to its human boat. In connec- tion with this spread of plague from the rat to man it is interesting to note that Homer, in describing the plague sent by Apollo on the Greeks, mattes it begin with the mules and dogs (Iliad I. 50).

===== 0002601_19081121_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTRICITY AND HORTICULTURE.

At Long Island, in the United States, an in- teresting experiznent he been attempted in introducing electriclty into horticulture. A Mrs. Davidoff, who has ,just • put this new method of culture to the test; says that her experiments have been made under frames or bell-glasses. She asserts that the flowers which she has obtained were very large, the perfumes very intense, and • the colour meal beautiful, far more so than in the Core of plants grown under glasd in ordinary condi- tions. A simple battery is all that she found tie ces6ary.

===== 0002601_19081121_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
SNOW AS MANURE.

In some parts of America the ides that snow is a manure has been widely accepted. The subject has been put to the test of scien- tific experiment in Ottawa, Canada, and the result she3vs that •there are only slight. grounds for the belief. It was found snow contains nitrogen in the form of free • ammonia, albuminoid ammonia, nitrates, and nitrites. The total, however, is ''-only '471, rather less than one-half, in a million. This was the average of twelve determinations, and means about lib. per acre from an average winter snowfall.

MAGNETIC TEMPERATURE GAUGE. An interesting little device, based on the discovery that every kind of carbon steel be- comes non-magnetic at the temperature proper for the operation of hardening, con- sists of a permanent horseshoe magnet, which is made to hold in the hand, with its two poles prolonged by rods of a spe- cial metal, which retains its magnetism above the hardening temperature of steel. The piece of steel to be hardened is placed en these prolonged poles and thrust into the blowpipe flame. As soon as the critical tem- perature is reached it loses its magnetism and drops into a vessel of brine placed to receive it. The device is only applicable for temper- ing small tools.

===== 0002601_19081121_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
MEASURING MOONLIGrT.

A hovel means of measuring moonlight is furnished by the selenium cell, through its well-known 'variations of electric resistance in light of different intensity. By this method the light of the full moon has been lately de- termined to be a little more than, onc-Elth of a standard candle-power, and about nine times the light of the half-ruocn. The gibbous (the shape of the moon between the full and the half) disc proves to be the brighter before than, after full mcon. The selenium cell is not perfectly reliable as a test for light of vat7hiff colour, but it lies been found suffici- ently auuraia find fi3nsitive to indicate the central phase of . a lunar eclipse within one minute of the computed time.

THE MILKY WAY.

The place occupied by the Milky Way in the theory of ths universe is continually in- creasing in importance. ThiS • vast belt of Stars is now regarded, not as a cluster com- parable with thousands of other clusters, but rather as the frame or skeleton of the entire stellar universe. Additional support for this view is derived, says the Srientilic American, from H. H. Turner's comparatire study oi variable stars or long period. Our sun is, to some extent, a variable stAr. The variations in its luminosity :Complete their cycle in a period of about cloven years during which the number of sun spots gradually increases from zero to a maximum and then decreases until the solar e.isc is ngain entirely spotle,s3. The solar activity is

MANIT'ESTED PrAtIODICALLT by the -spots and also by the intensely lumi- Polls • faculto which appear near the poles of the sun, mote toward the e-Jiator, and there vanish. It is 'evilent that the curve hich re- presents the Apparent triable luminosity of the sun will vary according to the position of the observer with reapect to" the solar axis, owing to the effects of perspective and ab- sorption by the solar atmosphere upon the ap- parent brightness of the equatorial faculea. Several astronomers have expressed tlio opinion that the

FLUCTUATIONS OF VATZTABLE STAIN (with the exception of stars of the Algol typo, in which the. variations are due to eclipses by a dark s!%tellite)•are similar in character to those of the sun, though vary different in de- gree. Turner, working •on this hypothesis, has examined the curves of variation of. A number of variable stars. • NEW LIFE-BQAT FOR ST. ANNES. The lloyai National Life-boat Institution has sent to 'St. Annes-on-the-Sen a new life boat to replace the smaller of the two life boats stationed there by the Institution many years ago. The new life-boat, which was built by the Thames Ironworks Company, is of the Liverpool tyre, 39ft. long by 9ft. broad ; she rows 12 oars, and is fitted with 2 water ballast tanks and 2 drop , keels. A new transporting carriage, the wheels of livhich are fitted with Tipping's Plateways, bus been supplied for nee with the new life-bont, together with a ' set of pushing poles, in order that the boat may be transported as rapidly as possible to the new launching ; place provided by the Institution at considerable I expense. The new life-boat is named the "James ISceriett," as desired by the late Mr. Imes carlett, of Bowdon, Cheshire, mho generously bequeathed a sum of , 11110ney to tbs Institution for a li le-hcat to bear this name to be stationed on the coast of ;Lancnshire. The , Committee of Management are much indebted to the ILocal Committee, and to Mr. Thomas Bradley, the Honorary Secretary, for their valuable co-operation in the management of the two life-boats at St. Annes-on- i the-Sen. The Royal National Life-Pont Institution has ' new 279 life-boats under its management, and not a winter passes without some of these boats rendering good service in saving life from shipwreck. 'ContriErr- tines in aid of the Institution will be gladly received by all the Bankers in the United Kingdom, by the several local Honorary Secretaries, and by Charles Dibdin. Esq., the Secretnry of the Institution, 22, I Charing Cross-road, London.

MEDICAL INSPECTION OF SCHOOLS. CHESHIRE'S HEAVY EXPENDITURE.

At a meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee, at Crewe, on Monday. a communication was read from the Beard of Education on the medical inspection of echocl children. The committee directed a reply expressing regret that the Board was still unable to indicate what financial assistance was to be given from imperial taxation towards the heavy expenditure which must he incurred if medial inspection of children is to be as effective NS it ought to he.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Are Votes Worth Having ?—Many p( °plc o ho °pp se wornans suffrage say, that women once given the vote would take no interest in the exercise of it. It is even I said that those women who lead the hysterical pro-' pfiganda of the day, and sacrifice peace and happiness for the prospect of a vote, would he found among the abstainers. That might be the case because it is n common human failing to want most those things we cannot get, and once attained they often cease to attract. But experience in the Colonies does not bear out the contention that if women were given the franchise they would care nothing about it. In Australia, all British women who have resided six months in the country have the vrte for the Common- wealth Parliament, and in the 1006 election about 41 women and 56 men recorded their votes out of every possible 100 electors respectively. In the voting for the separate State Parliaments, the difference in the proportion of voters is much less, so that it cannot be said that women regard the vote as useless, and in some of the Anstralian States, women have had the vote for the past tael ve or fourteen years so that it is far from being a novelty. Tie Queen's Gift Book.—" Qceen Alexandra's Christmas Gift Book: Photographs from my Camera" is full of pictures of human interest, and many give us the note of Boyd family life and amusements. "My two grandson's wading ' illustrates the way Iler Majesty has given a Lrief description to each picture, end there are snapshots of landscapes, sporting groups, navel and military subiects, scenes and ceremonies of various descriptions taken in the Highlands, in Norway, Denmark, the Mediterranean, and elsewhere. It is a took of 130 paces, well bound in a dainty cover of white canvas, and is sold for half-a-crown for the benefit of hospitals and other charities in which Her Majesty is interested. The sale has been so large that it is said to have adversely affected the demand for the ordinary kind of Christmas gift books, but in view of the fact that the profits go to relieve suffering ard winter dis- tress, it is to be hoped that the public will not allow the interests of authors, artists aril publishers to suffer

to any great extent on this account. Royal Snapsbotters.—The publication of a selection of Queen Alexandra's photographs, is not the first time , her Majesty has allowed the public to judge of her 1 proficiency in the handling of the camera. Some time ' ago,a leading photographic company gave an exhibition of a series ef enlargements of original negatives of Royal photographers, end among them were some twenty-form charming studies contribnted by the Queen. Her Majesty'm camera pictures invariably dis- play great artistic taste in view-finding, and in these exhibits as also in the selectinn of pictures in the Queen's "Christmas Gift Book " there are evidences of real photographic skill. Her Majesty's enthusiasm for the camera is shared by several other members of the Royal Family and particularly by Princess Victoria, and Princess Henry of Battenberg. King Edward is not a devotee cf the camera,. hit his Majesty has none of the dislike with which Royalty used to view ; " anapshotting " when it first became popular. The i intrusion of the " snapshotter " was not altogether welcome. but opinions have so far changed that TIOR here is the camera more a source of delight than in the ltoaal palaces. The Deserted Drawing-Room.—With both middle- . class and wealthy people, the somewhat frigid drawing- mom seems to he going out of favonr. Originally "with-drawing " room was the name and function of this apartment. and modern social customs are inclined to rebel against its traditions. Many of the leading hotels, not only in London end elsewhere, have magnifi- cent drawing-rooms, but, as n rule they are- deserted apartments. The man avoid them because they cannot smoke there, and the women find them dull. Both seek the more lively associations of the lounge, where there is smoking and music, and there is the same tendency to improvise a sort of half-way meeting place between dining and drawing-rooms in nrivatehouses. Whenever one enters a drawing-room that has been unreformed since the mid Victorian era, one almost eapects to meet unpleasant people who will sneer, " rut on airs," and take a delight in hurting one feelings. There is a depressing atmosphere about the room, which most people naturally have an inclination to avoid. Social Amenities.—As compared with the mid- I Victorian era, we have improved in vast number of ways. erineation; in manners, in dress, and all spheres of physical and mental culture we have iraprcved, but whether we are more sociable, with these other advantages, is r. matter for doubt. Colonial visitors invariably compinie that as a people we are I afflicted with the spirit of aloofnese. That, it it to be feared, is true. Tt is a national characteristic, and is responsible for ranch of the social isolation in lahich so many pass their lives. Mrs. kis painfully select, and easily offended ; Mrs. B it aggressive, and always find- ing !atilt; Mr, C is shallow and insincere; and Mrs D is polite becsoge the Lopos to gain by it. A SMART WINTER HAT.

One of the features of the present season's fashions is the amount of fur used by the fashionable milliners on the majority of their smartest creations. • The betoming hat pic- tured in our sketch is one of these modish fur-trimmed chapeaux. The original, which came from a' very famous French house, was carried, out in plum coloured Ottoman -silk, the spreading, slightly upturned brim being

lined with satin of the same shade. All round the huge and rather low crown WM placed a big pointo-fox skin, WillCh Witß .so arranged that it made a complete circle 'with- out shewing a join anywhere. At the bottom of this skin was disposed a wreath of small silver roses of silk and velvet. .. Some of the petals of these 'roses were slightly frosted, whilst others were faintly shaded, the effect being indescribab:y pretty as well as unusu- ally smart and distingue.

A MODISH COSTUME.

This Costilrile h carried out iu fine smooth faced cloth, of fairly heavy weight, in a beautiful tone of smoke-grey: The coat is cut in close semi-fitting style, and has just the Merest sus- picion of a short-waisted effect at the back. Cranby Vane sat by his window, looking out at the children .playing in the gardens of Gray's Inn. The imps of the gutter were sprawling .on the grass, running races round the historic tree beneath which Bacon is supposed to have sat, and doing their best to elude the vigilance of the grey-haired beadle, who, watch in hand, waited for the clock of the Holborn Town Hall to knell out the hour which doomed the children to return to their native slums. Vane did not distinguish any particular child in the heap; they seemed a confused, happy, rolling, dirty mass of bodies, legs, and arms, all inextricably mixed up on the green grass, whilst the Gray's Inn rooks, froth the tops of the trees, cawed solemn disapproval of this indeeorous scene. Ho was frying to get away from the effect of a let- ter which he held in his hand—a letter which caused him to bite his long moustache and wish there were no women in the world.

He flnn; the litter on the ground in a crumpled heap; then picked it up and read it again. It was a ,curicus document, and one cal- culated to cause uneasiness'—to any man who had once loved a girl as well as Vane had loved Ewcretta Gilchrist. LANCASHIRE TRAINING COLLEGE.

&flinty Committee's Proposal Carried By One Vote. At a meeting of the Lancashire Education Committee on Monday, cons'derahle discussion took place regard- ing a proposal that the committee should build with or without the co-operation of the other edecatiost authorities in the country, the training college for the accommodation of 153 female teachers. Mr. Kinch contended that no justification existed for such a building. There was an overplus of trhined

teachers. Mr. Bradbury pointed out that there were 19,01 supplementary teachers in the couutry, as com Pare,, with 6,CLO seven years ago, and consequently twine'

teaches were r male to iftwi situations. - • Archde con Fletcher urged that it would be batter t° turn away „young people now, when they had a chauo of finding another profession, than that they should allowed to continue their studies, only to find the chance of appointment was hopeless. The motion Wtl3 carried by 20 votes to 19.

PLAYING WITH FIRE. Mr. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquest at St; Helens on Tuesday on Daniel Dillon, th 9 two year ola son of a miner. of .Napier-street, who died in the Providence Hospital on Thursday last from burns received the previous Sunday. The father said that 00 Sunday, the Bth, he was in charge of the house and the three children, aged six and two years, and the LI aged seven weeks. lie went upstairs for a paper, ao hearing a scream, ran down and found Daniel in flames' 110 was at once attended to. Mr. Furness : You can prosecute by showing tie damage which is done. Members of the Council expressed sympathy with the farmers' complaint but it was pointed out that the Council had no authority to do anything. It was suggested that a memorial should be drawn up to bo signed by the various farmers of the district warning the harriers against any further damage,other- wise they would prosecute, and this should be sent to the secrotaries or various clubs;

Mr. .Fairclo4h and the Chairmen (who are both farmers) said they would adopt this course, as they were determined to pat down a practice which led to so much loss to the farmers of the district.

SCEPTICISM TURNED TO PRAISE.

WELL-KNOWN MANCHESTER MAN'S STRONG VOLUNTARY STATEMENT PRAISING DR. MORSE'S INDIAN ROOT PILLS.

Mr. George Paterson, of 109, New Lane, Patricroft, Manchester, sends the strong state- ment published below, because he was so satisfied himself with the results of taking Dr. Morse's Indian Root Pills, and he wanted to make them better known by adding his testimony. Mr._ Paterson writes :

"You are strangers to me and I to you; and a deep feeling of gratitude is the only excuse I can offer for presuming to send you these few lines. Fsor many years I have been a victim to disordered liver and its consequent afflictions, and nearly three years ago was induced to try Dr. Morse's Indian Root Pills, a medicine I can now conkientiously assert which in every way

accomplishes all thit yo.o claim for it. I must admit at first I was some-

what sceptical, being all my.. life abhorrent of

medicines and drugs of any description, but now

after a fair three years'

trial of Dr. Morse's Indian

Root Pills, I frankly admit that they kave

worked wonders on me,

and have, practically speaking, made quite a new man o. Without doubt they are honestly what you represent them to-be, and that is saying a very great deal in this advanced scientific 'age in which we live. So thoroughly satisfied am lof their astonishing success, I consider such a boon to suffering humanity should be made as well known as possible •, therefore you have my full liberty to make whatever use you may think fit of this letter. I may state that I still con- tinue taking two Pills every night before going to bed, and will do so as long as I can get them. . . . .

The above testimonial, tol'a in a siinple, con- scientious, matter-of-fact manner, bears the ring of truth. It is an unvarnished narrative of the wonderful efficacy of Dr. Morse's Indian Root Pills to overcome the common ailments of mankind. Dr. Morse's Indian Root Pills get at the cause of pain and sickness. They drive the impurities out of the blood. They give you clean bowels, a healthy stomach, a lively liver, active kidneys,, and pure blood, rich and red, imparting health and strength to the whole system. For all complaints arising from Impure Blood and a disordered state of the Stomach, Liver and Kidneys, these Pills are unexcelled. Sold by Chemists and Stores, price 1/11 per bottle, or The W. H. Comstock Co., Ltd., 21, Parringdon Avenue, London, E.C.

Chas. FORDEs ESILE 13€11/41S

"PROPHECY AND IhSPIRATION."

The first of two lectures on "Prophecy and Inspiration" arranged by the Warrington Church Society, was delivered in Holy frinity schools on Wednesday evening by the Rev. Canon Joyce, warden of St. Deiniol's, Ilawarden. The Rev. Canon Stevenson presided over a good attendance. The lecturer said the inspiration of the Bible was one of those foundation facts upon which the Christiln faith rested. At a time like this when so many fundamental questions of religion Were discussed in the freest possible way,it was of the first raportance that they should have clear con- ceptions an definite thoughts upon these great matters. Moreover in -uch questions they could not be content to refer to the views held in the past, because a large amount of new knowledge and information was coming to the front and it demanded that they should adapt their conceptions and read-just them possibly in some measure. Inspired ..ri was the communication between the Divine spirit and the human spirit. The prophets were especially and ins, peculiar degree the men of inspir- ation. All the authors of the Bible were in their degree inspired but we must recognize different degrees of inspiration. The three sources from which he proposed to derive information on the subject were (I) the Bible itself, standing pre-eminent above the rest ' • (2) comparison between the different religions of the world ; (3) the phsychology of religion and revelation. , The lecturer proceeded to show how the Israelites, as l well as the heathen races, practised divination and be submitted that the explanation was to be found in the recognition of certain mysterious powers which belonged to human nature as such and which in the present day received CLITiOUS names like telepathy, the power of thought-reading and thought-transference, , and the power of perceiving things at a distance. Divination in the case of the heathen sank lower and lower into a pit of degradation, while in the ci,se of Israel it TOSO higher and higher until it passed into prophecy. One great reason for this was that divination amongst the heathen was connected with a degrading and debased form of polytheism and idolatry, whereas divination amongst the Hebrews was connected with the worship of the one true God. That made an immense and immeasurable difference. All the weak- nesses and evils of polytheism registered them- rel ves in the practice of divination, whereas divination in Israel became more and more purified by its connection with the worship of God. Divination amongst the heathen was degraded by the ambition, love of power,and covetousness of its professors, but the diviners of Israel were conspicuously free from those two great besetting sins. The prophets used their powers to the highest ends and noblest pur- poses and so it was that they prepared the way for the corning of the Son of God. The RECTOR moved a vote of thanks to the lecturer. Mr. 0. Losmouse seconded and said be WAS parti- cularly struck with the lecturer's reference to the fact that the divination of the heathen races was not chicanery and falsehood. We were living in an age when we were learning to believe that there were powers outside us of which we had not known,and some people were able to take advantage of them. No doubt in those early days the diviners were men like some people living to-day who were able to get beyond the bounds which circumscribed most of us. The vote was heartily passed.

Tortured by Disfiguring Scaly Sores.

Miss E. B. Haddock, of 30, Hampden-street,Westoe, South Shields, had more torture crowded into two years than thousands of people have in a lifettme. Nothing was able to ielieve her of the terrible eczema until Zara-Bak was tried. To a local reporter who investigated the wonderful eure Miss Haddock said :—" About two years sac) I noticed a number of small red spots - like a rash

appearing all over my body, face, head, arms, and legs J called in the doctor, who told me I had eczema. As there were children at the house where I was employed, I was obliged to glee up my sitiurtion. " Doctor's medicine did me no good, for the spots soon began to spread .!Ito each other. "Two of the places festered and began to discharge. Ity body was very sore, and the skin was highly inflamed. The ;te/ting and pain were almost unbear- able. I could not sleep, but used to walk about all night. " The eczema continued to spread and I bream' an;., ully disfigured. Before I was attacked by the disease I bad a lot of thick bushy hair, which came nearly nearly down to the waist. The eczema caused the hair to come out in handfuls, until there was only a thin covering left. The doctor told me there was no cure for me.

"Many a time I cried bitterly, for I never had a moment's rest from the burning, torturing pains. For two years my body was prcctically covfred with the dreadful eczema, and there seemed no prospect of ever getting rid of it. When the disease was about at its worst, I was advised by several friends to try Zam-Buk.

" I smeared the balm over the scaly places gently. The scabs began to peel off, and a new healthy sh;n grew where the sores had been. I continued with Zatn-Buk, until my head, face, neck, hotly, arms, and legs, were quite cleared of the dreadful eczema. My skin is now beautifully scit and healthy looking, and my hair is growing again." Miss fladdocl's case conclusively proves the um-quailed excellence nf

The interest of the sitna- THE ECLIPSE OF tion in Germany deepens

ABSOLUTISM. rather than otherwise. It is a constitutional crisis of first-rate importance, tided over for the time being no doubt, but still full of possibilities, and certain to have enduring consequences. None of us expected that events would develops quite in the direction they have taken. Even those who were most well-informed on German affairs would not have ventured to predict such an uprising of opinion against the proceedings of the EMPEROR as has actually manifested itself. There is the widest possible difference in the views expressed by the Garman Press on the subject of recent events, but through them all can be dis- cerned the breaking out of a long restrained impatience with the EMPEROR'S naturaldisposition to meddle with everything in the spirit of one who believes himself to be a past master in all varieties of human affairs. Discontent has been simmering under an apparently quiet surface. What seemed like national admiration has been a cloak to cover an inflamed national sense of the EMPEROR'S indiscretion. Obviously the KAISER has himself known nothing of this. If he has learned of all that has been said about him in the Reichstag and elsewhere since the appearance of the famous interview, be must be at this moment the most disillusioned man in Europe. But of course he has only been permitted to learn what it was con- sidered necessary for him to know. Because he did not know too much his reconciliation with his Chancellor seems to have become quite easy. What is proceeding in Germany, it seems to us, is a quiet revolution. Without noise, without Sturm and Drang (as the Germans themselves might say), the popular will is gradually asserting itself as the controlling power in the State. Whilst the KAISER has seemed to be drifting towards absolutism, the people, not only of Prussia, but of Germany at large, have been thinking of constitu- tionalism in its broader and more enlightened forms. On the future method of government the "Daily Telegraph" interview has given them an opportunity of expressing opinions which must for a long time past have been suppressed with some difficulty,as may be guessed from the sullen 1 and determined energy with which they now find expression. Not even in Germany, as it now clearly appears, is this a favourable time for any attempt to revive personal government. The people simply would not have it. They are deter- mined not only that the Sovereign shall not act without his ministers, but that Ministers shall be responsible not only to the S wereign but to their own representatives. The attainment of this second object is not as yet a question of the moment, it May be, but it is probably not a very long way off. It may be reserved for Germany, to do by perfectly quiet and orderly means what some other countries have only accomplished by violent revolutions.

The EMPEROR himself, it must be admitted, is behaving admirably. His treatment of PRINCE BUELOW could scarcely be bettered. He has acted as if he were the pardoning and forgiving party, and yet without undue ostentation, and even, as it would seem, with a certain gentleness. It is ' probable that he has promised his Chancellor not to give any further interviews to gentlemen of the Press, either English or American, but he has done this as if he were receiving the Chancellor's own submission. He has eaten the leek whilst appearing to present it to others. He wears his stigma like a badge. It is a fine piece of comedy, after the manner of Moliere. But the EMPERoR. has really given up nothing except his liberty to talk at large without previously consulting his Ministers. The great constitutional questions remain where they were. As KING of Prtussri he may be absolutist if he likes. It is by a kind of condescension that he permits Parliamentary institutions to exist. They are useful to keep the people quiet, but they have no power to override the Sovereign's will. Now that a movement towards genuine Constitution- alism has set in, there presents itself a problem that is deeply interesting to the world at large. How will the EMPEROR behave ? Will he (to use Mr. CHAMBERLA IN'S favourite phraee) "take it lying down F"

The Borough Member gave THE MEMBER further evidence of his keen

AND SPORT.

interest in sport on Satur- day, for in addition to witnessing the great struggle betwen Warrington and the Australians at Wilderspool, he made an appropriate speech of welcome to the Colonial visitors at the subsequent convivial gathering and later presided at the annual prize distribution in connection with the Warriugton and District Bowling Association, of which he is the president. Mr. Crosfield's speech at each gathering was most interesting and the Australians appeared pleased with his well-merited tribute to the way in which their Colony is coming to the fore, not only in different branches of sport but in more serious walks of life. The Member kias always been a warm supporter of bowls and in his remarks before the Bowling Association he dwelt upon the phenomenal progress of that pastime and also had something to say about its great antiquity. Local trundlers would probably be surprised to hear that even in the days of Richard 11. bowls was fast becoming a craze in this country and such dire consequences were feared that a law was passed to discourage it. The game was never more popular than it is to-day, and it is a I pleasing sign of the times that workingmen are disposed to devote so much of their spare time to this healthful outdoor recreation rather than to sitting in the public-house.

The third reading of the WHAT WILL THE Licensin2 Bill was carried LORDS D 3 P in the House of Commons

yesterday evening by 350 votes to 113,—majority 237. This is surely emphatic enough evidence of the conviction held by the people's representatives that licensing reform is imperative and that the Governmeat Bill is a necessary piece of legislation. We can imagine the Trade offering up the heartfelt prayer: Good Lords deliver us I But if the Commons are to count for anything in the Government of the country their mandate must be obeyed when it , comes with such force and directness. The Lords will do well to pause and rtflact before they fly in the face of the country on such a matter as licensing reform. SD many of them have not clean hands that a rejection of the Bill now to be submitted from the Lower House can only inflame the anger and stiffen the will of the people. Sir Henry .Campbell-Bannerman, of revered memory, warned the Lords that "the will of the people shall prevail," and such a warning had best be heeded in a crisis like the present. The people are liable on provocation, to display • intolerance of their hereditary legislators. The Premier, in moving the third reading, expounded the main arguments for the Bill in masterly style. S.) far as reasoned logic can carry weight he stated an unanswerable case. He pointed out that no less than six weeks of Parlia- mentary time had been spent on the Bit—there- fore it cannot be contended for a moment in " another place" that it is an ill-considered measure. Mr. Asquith challenged criticism: Was it disputed that there was an excess of licences as compared with reasonable requirements, and could anyone suggest a more equitable way of getting rid of the redundant licences than that which had been put forward by the Govern- ment P It was common ground in all parties of the State that the community carght to have the right of mouopaly value. If the contention now put forward, that the interest in a licence was a freehold, were correct—and here the Premier gave the Tories and the Trade a poser—why was it that in Mrupalfour's 1904 Act compensation was payable noOty the commanity but by the Trade? Finally, Mr. Asquith said that the Bill was the first serious and solid attempt in our time to pass a measure of temperance reform. Whatever might be its fate elsewhere it was sufficient for him that it represented the considered judgment of the Commons.

Nothing in the world can Tau LZAGUE OF quite take the place of PERSONAL SERVICE. personal service, and the appeal of Mrs. Asquith, Mrs. Gladstone, the Duke of Norfolk, Llrd Salisbury, Mr. (Jerald Balfour, Mr. Arthur Henderson, and the rest for volunteers who will undertake to visit their poorer neighbours in London represents a need more general and profound than any hard- ship of the season in crowded centres. There is no help in the world like human relationship, and the vital communication of sympathy. But no work needs more knowledge, character, tact and enthusiasm. The scheme consists in setting the workers each to look after a small group of house- holds under a system devised by the Central (Unemployed) thdy and the Charity Organisation Society. The visitation by itself is excellent, but the visitor will find many ways of practically befriending h's new acquaintat.ces, and not the least of the values of the scheme should be a quickened sense of individual responsibility to one's immediate neighbours in all the ordinary affairs of life. One may get to know the struggling local trader better, and ignore the Parisian house. London has no peculiar need for such a league of service which other places do not share. Every urban centre and some rural places require the wise organisation of personal ministration. The example of the London Committee is an admirable one and deserves to be followed in many directions.

THE POLITICS OF THE WEEK.

[Fitom OUR LONDON CORRESPONDENT.]

A week devoted almost exclusively to the Licensing Bill has been dominated by an anxious desire for " intelligent anticipation" of the future. What will be tbe fate of the Bill in the Upper House ? Will the Lords sacrifice social reform to purely party con- siderations, and ruthlessly throw out the measure on the second reading? And speculation has been not less busy with the chances of success attending the compromise which the Government have been strenuously labouring to establish in the Education _controversy. On Wednesday and Thursday of last week the Port of London Bill was carried through the Committee stage. On the latter day, too, Mr. Asquith made an important statement in relation to the Govern- ment's view of the Two Power standard, and a motion to adjourn the House in order to discuss a circular issued by the Local Government Board was met by a frank declaration from the Prime Minister that the intention of his promised concessions to the Unem- ployed had been misconstrued by the Board. The annual Conference of the National Union of Conserva- tive and Constitutional Associations, which met this week at Cardiff, reaffirmed that " practical unity of purpose" in the pursuit of Tariff Reform to which the Party is always being " finally committed." But in spite of this finality the fact remains, as the " Morning Post" pointed out in a pained and bewildered manner, that Mr. Balfour barked discussion of Tariff Reform in the debate upon Unemployment.

===== 0002601_19081121_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FLAW- IN THE -OPPOSITION'S CASE.

There is a funadmental weakness in the case of the Opposition against the- Licensing Bill which neither dialectical skill nor vehement denunciation can con- ceal. This appeared very clearly in the verbal duel between Mr. Balfour and Mr. Herbert Samuel on Monday. It is impossible for Mr. Balfour to maintain that the property in a licence is freehold. Not only would the contention be too flagrantly contradicted by the authorities of the Law Courts, but it is barred by unambiguous testimony of the Act of 1901. If the property in a licence is on the same footing as property in railways or land, why did that Act raise com- pensation on the surviving licences, and not out of public funds ? Mr. Samuel left the Leader of the Opposition no loophole by which he could escape from the confession that a time-limit in itself was not open to objection. The Opposition refuse to entertain the idea of a time-limit at all, and yet they cannot deny that, even iron their point of view, it only requires certain conditions to make a time-limit equitable. They protest that compensation which is not based on the principle of an absolute property in a licence is sheer robbery ; yet they themselves, when in office passed a measure to make licence-holders pay their own compensation. WHO HAS BEEN SHAKING CREDIT? Lord Lansdowne used language about the Licensing Bill at the Junior Carlton Club last week which Mr. Birrell has rightly described as "ominous." It bore a sinister significance, coming from the master of many legions in the House of Lords. And he and Mr. o oar Law have both been talking with a great show ° earnestness about the shock which the Government

has given to credit. As far as the shock has bean the result of acts, it is by giving pensions to the aged poor and by reducing the sugar-tax, 80 these ingenious critics would suggest, that the Government has injured credit. But it is by words rather than by deeds, they insinuate, that the injury has been done. This mis- chievous nonsense has gone on far too long. If any feeling of insecurity has really been engendered, it is the restless calumnies of the Opposition that are wholly responsible. They are quite ready to shake public confidence in the Government by attributing to it the wildest and most improbable designs, trust- ing that the Government and not they will be credited with any harm that may ensue. But this Government, which they represent as the enemy of commerce and industry, have proved their desire and capacity to benefit British trade by such legislation as the Patents Act, by grappling with the problem of reorganizing the Port of London, and by averting or putting an end to formidable conflicts of interest between Capital and Labour. In three years the present Government have accomplished more for industrial efficiency and peace than than the Adminis- trations of Lord Salisbury and Mr. Balfour even attempted in a decade.

A MISUNDERSTANDING REMOVED.

The misunderstanding which centred round a Local Government Board circular to Distress Committees, and which led to a motion by Mr. Henderson for the adjournment of the House on Thursday, the 12th, was easily removed. Mr. Asquith, in making his state- ment of what the Government proposed to do for unemployment, announced that the two disqualifica- tions involved in having received Poor Relief, or of having been engaged upon relief work during either of the two preceding months, would be removed in order that deserving cases might be relieved by the Distress Committee. The Local Govern- ment Board circular instructed the Distress Committees that they would be free to use their dis- cretion in regard to these disqualifications. The Labour Party drew attention, through Mr. Henderson's motion for adjournment, to a discrepancy that was apparently of a very pronounced character. In reality, the difficulty was mainly a matter of phraseology. The effect of the circular was to draw the attention of the Committees to two facts; first, that applicants we no longer disqualified, as they had been, on the score of having received Poor Relief, or having been employed on relief works ; secondly, that, of course, this change did not absolve the Com- mittees from the obligation of discriminating between deserving and undeserving cases. But Mr. Asquith very properly hastened, as soon as his attention was called to this misconception that had arisen, to make it perfectly clear that his promise would be fulfilled both in the letter and the spirit. The Labour Party immediately recognised the generous spirit in which he was acting.

===== 0002601_19081121_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
A GREAT HOPE.

When a compromise is being negotiated in a con- troversy of long standing that has its roots in deep strata of prejudice and principle, it is never safe to be sanguine even in the last stage of it all. Each party to the controversy regards the concessions that have already been made, not as a reason why one more and final concession should be made, but as a cumulative reason against any further movement. It would be rash to hope too much from the remarkable progress that has been made towards an effective compromise in the Education controversy. It is, however, of the greatest importance that the public should realise to the full the enormous benefit which would accrue from a settlement of the embittered disputes that have been so long impairing national efficiency by. making a truly national system of education. It is a great achievement on Mr. Runciman's part to have carried matters so far, and if the enormous boon of educa- tional efficiency should now at last be proved to have been reached through educational peace, then, as Lord Crewe said at the Colston banquet, it would be ingratitude to forget the yeoman service that Mr. Birrell did to the same cause by his tact, fairness,and impariality, when he was at the Board of Education, although his efforts were frustrated by extremists in the House of Lords. Successful compromises are rarely achieved except over the wreck of others that just failed of success.

GREAT BRITAIN'S NECESSITY. The memorandum of Mr. Alfred Marshall on Free Trade and Tariff Reform, which has been published in a Parliamentary paper, deserves the most careful con- sideration from all thoughtful men. Mr. Marshall, admittedly one of the greatest, perhaps the greatest, of living economists, is neither a partisan nor a pedant. He judges economic policy by practical results, not by the degree to which it conforms to economic formula. There are three points in his memorandum which are specially notable. First, he discovers in the process by which high-minded men have defended a preferential policy an inclination to ignore the urgent practical reasons which commend it to its strongest supporters—the material advantages it gives to one section of the community. Here, he says, is a dangerous opening for self-deception. Secondly, without laying down a dogmatic, absolute rule, he affirms that, as a practical result, the con- sumer pays the import duties. Lastly, and most important of all, he declares that the industrial well- being of Great Britain is dependent on special con- ditions which make Free Trade necessary for its existence.

Visitor in X-ray department of hospital to Radio- grapher (who has answered many questions): " What would you see if you turned the X•rays on a dog's chest ? Weary Radiog,rapher : "The seat of his pants." * *

It was startling news to the audience at the missionary meeting at Wycliffe Memorial Hall, on Tuesday evening, to hear from a lady who has worked in Samoa that the natives of those islands were exceedingly fond of Epsom salts. How tastes differ!

Big Bill Taft ! the New President of the United States, is said to combine physical bulk with native politeness in an uncommon degree. It is related of him that he once rose from his seat in a tramcar to allow three ladies to sit down. Bill is a great man, but they wouldn't have him in the Metropolitan Fire Brigade! * * *

The Education Committee's sprats bid fair to make full-sized mackerel at an early date. During the past season, 800 boys from fifteen public elementary schools in the Borough have attended at the public baths once a week, and they have all learnt to swim to some extent, 200 of them being able to swim at least one length, 150 at least one breadth, and the remainder at least half a breadth. * * *

The Borough Member's ambitions are not all realised yet. Addressing the members of the Bowling Association on Saturday evening, Mr. Crosfield. said : " I don't mind telling you," " that if ever my ship comes in—(laughter)—and I can afford to do a little bit on my own, I have at the back of my mind a little project of a pretty brown trout stream where those silvery finny darters abound, in that bonny land of Scotland—in fact, a small stream on the west coast there, and a turn at the sea trout when they are running after a spate in July, comes about as near my ideal of human happiness as anything I know of." If, when his ship comes in, Mr. Crosfield does indulge in a trout stream on the West Coast of Scotland we hope the cargo will e large enough also to allow a golf course near Warrington. With a " town" house and a trout stream offering their inducements to Mr. Crosfield, we should need something stronger even than the perfume at Bank Quay to attract him to Warrington as often as he would be welcome. We are not, of course, overlooking the fact that he might still be Member for Warrington in addition. * *

An air of excitement pervaded our local Parliament on Thursday evening for it was known that the fate of the Government was trembling in the balance. As events turned out, the Right to Work Bill filled the majority of the hon. members with such alarm that the Ministry were defeated. But they were not dis- graced, for only by a single vote were their proposals rejected. There will be no dissolution. The Govern- ment will continue to hold the reins of power until the end of the session, when the Tories will have a chance of showing what they can do. We hope they will lose no time in embodying their tariff reform views, which they are so fond of proclaiming at every conceivable opportunity, in the form of a Dili, for Free Traders are always prepared to buckle on the armour and defend their cause in debate. At the same time, if the Tories desire to retain office long, they will be well advised in relegating tariff reform to a back place for, as far as can be judged, the House does not appear to be enamoured of this panacea for all our ills. However, we shall see.

In view of the inquiry into the explosion at the Maypole Cannel Mine in the Wigan coalfield a corres- pondent of the "Morning Post" recalls the fact that the Wigan district has been famous for its cannel coal for over 200 years. In olden times it was called candle coal, from the fact that before gas was utilised as an illuminant it served on winter evenings for candle light as well as coal fire. It is a hard, splinter- ing coal, and the colliers hewing it are covered with blue marks, almost like indistinctly pricked tattoo figures. It was the custom in the olden times to turn this coal in the lathe and make all manner of curious carvings in coal. Visitors to Wigan were accustomed to see a variety of quaint articles sculptured out of the hard cannel coal of the district by local workmen, who were artists after their fashion. The coal took a polish as fine as black marble, • and there is a record to show that more than two centuries ago a present of dressing-boxes made of Cannel coal was sent to the Queen of Prussia from Wigan.

* * Many of our readers will recall the articles written by Mr. Henry Whitham about the corruption and "graft " for which San Francisco has become notorious. With a new city, efforts are being 'nide to bring about a purer administration, and one of the first steps was the arrest of Abraham Ruef, the Mayor, on charges which we should describe here as " using his public position very much to his private advantage. Mr. Heney, the assistant district attorney of San Francisco, is one of a group of public-spirited men, and it is in this cause of municipal purity that he has suffered violence at the hands of a scoundrel who shot him at close quarters whilst sitting in the court during the trial of Abraham Ruef, and afterwards committed suicide in prison. Mr. Heney had an almost miraculous escape from instant death, for the shot was fired at his head within a distance of three feet. The latest accounts give strong hopes that he will recover from his wound and be able to carry on his compaign against the forces of corruption. * * * Groat Sankey farmers are up in arms against the harriers. Their complaint is that these fleet-footed sportsmen do damage to crops and fences in the district and7so seriously do they regard the question that Mr. H. Fairclough, himself a farmer, brought it forward at the meeting of the Sankey Parish Council on Monday evening. Of course, the Council was powerless to take action but the farmers were advised to memorialise the harriers' clubs and if this had not the desired effect to prosecute in cases where damage could be proved. We are certain that the harriers will view the matter in a reasonable light and that if there is just ground for the complaint they will see that it is removed. Cross-country running is a healthy sport and worthy of every encouragement but the farmers must be treated with consideration and doubtless the local clubs will be only too willing to respect their rights. . .

* * * The " Morning Post" is again lamenting that tbe Opposition will not attack the Government on unem- ployment by preaching Tariff Reform as the cure. ILL a leading article on the subject we read that this policy mght involve temporary co-operation with the Labour members, whose ideas of a remedial policy are of a contrary order " : Yet it is surely not against the traditions of British politics for parties or groups which have nothing else in common to unite for the purpose of turning out a Government they agree ill distrusting. Ths test, however, of this sort of strategy is whether it succeeds. We cannot imagine the Labour party or Socialists forming an unholy alliance with the Tariff Reformers to turn out the Government. Why, even Victor Grayson would prefer a Liberal Governmen to a Tariff Reform regime. The "Morning Post must try again!

* * * The new standing order of the Town Council which prohibits any member from being chairman or deputy chairman of more than two Committees came into operation this week when the Committee appointments were made. There have been few changes which may be considered drastic, but we hardly think the Sanitary Works Committee has gained by the sub- stitution of Councillor Sankey as chairman, and Councillor Wilson as deputy-chairman for Alderman John Evans and Alderman Smethurst. Whatever qualifications they have displayed in Committee, the two first-named gentlemen have hardly distinguished themselves in the Council meetings except by main- taining a discreet silence when contentious topics have been on the carpet. Alderman Evans has rendered very valuable service as chairman of the Sanitary Works Committee, and without his guidance one cannot have quite the same confidence in this important department's efficiency. The Great Wall of Chin: was measured in many places by Mr. lintlank, an American engineer lately engaged on a survey for a Chinese railway. His measurements give the height at 18ft., and a width on top of 15ft. Every few hundred yards there is a tower 25ft. square and from 201 t. to 451 t. high. The foundation of the wall is solid granite. Mr. Unthank brought with him a brick from the wall, which is supposed to have been made 200 years before the Itime of Christ. In building this immense stone fence to keep out the Tartars, the builders never attempted to avoid mountains or chasms to save expense. For 1,300 miles the wall goes over plain and mountain, and every foot of the found- ation is in solid granite and the rest of the structure solid masonry. In some places the wall is built smooth up against the bank, or canyons, or precipi i ces, when there s a sheer descent of I,oooft. Small streams are arched over, but in the larger streams the wall runs to the water's edge and a tower is built on each side. On the top of the wall there are breastworks, or defences, facing in and out, so the defending force can pass from one tower to the other without being exposed to any enemy from either side. To calculate the time of building or cost of this wall is beyond human skill. So far as the magnitude of the work is concerned, it surpasses everything in ancient or modern times of which there is any trace. The pyramids of Egypt are nothing compared to it.

-Gruesome Discovery in the River. The headless body of a man, in an advanced state of decomposition was discovered on Sunday afternoon by Police-canstable Corlett, on a sandbank, in the Mersey, at Pen keth. The left foot was missing, and the clothing is unrecognisable. The height of the man is, apparently, 51t. Gin. There was a boot on -the right foot, and a black leather cigarette case and a dirty silk handkerchief were found in the clothing. The body appears to have been buried in the sand about twelve months. Warrington Co-operative Society. The Educational Committee of the above Society held the second of their educational meetings on Thursday night in Bold-street khool which was crowded to its utmost capacity. Mr. W. Haigh pre- sided, and spoke of the progressive work of the society and its rapid increase of members and business. He alluded to the boot department, which taking the returns of the last 10 months, was £1,700 in advance of the corresponding 10 months of last year. They were prepared to do a great deal more, with the 9,000 members at their backs, and he hoped for an even greater loyally to the society's work. The Principal item of the evening was the re-appearance - of Mr.Barnish Barnsdale, the elacutionist,of Rochdale, who, as upon previous occasions, kept the large gathering spell-bound. The hour and a half recital seemed as a few minutes, so intense was the interest taken in his delineations. The Co-operative employees' Male Voice Choir sang two pieces very creditably : "In absence " and " Crusaders." Mr. aohn W. Willett sang, " Ailva Mine " and "The Evening Song "in very pleasing style, and for an encore gave " The Sailor's Grave." Mr. Willett is quite a favourite at these concerts and his items were well received. Miss Jessie Farrar was the accompanist The chairman was accompanied on the platform by Mr. Thomas Wadsworth (president of the society), Mr. T 3. Farrar (chairman of the Educational Committee), J. Jarman (secretary of the society), Mr. Cook and Mr. Rushton (members of the Educational Committee.) 'Tea and Concert at Legh-street. Warrington Philomathic Society. . • - • •

A meeting of the above Societ; was held in Gregory's Cafe on Wednesday. A mock municipal election took place, which was both interesting and amusing. There were three candidates, a Conserva- tive, an Independent, and a Socialist, each of whom held a separate meeting and had his own chairman. The audience was composed of 'members of the Society who were allowed at the close of each meeting to put questions to the candidate. The members voted for the candidate who, in their opinion, delivered the best speech ; this resulted in the Independent being elected Penny Popular Concerts. _

At the Warrington County Court on Thurshy, before his Honour Judge Shiress Will, K.O , George Brown, commercial traveller, Ash Cottage,Lymrn,sued. the International Tool Supply Co., Birmingham, for £5O salary and commission alleged to be due from Ist July to date and damages for breach of agreement. —Mr. Browne appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. Dowsett, barrister (instructed by Messrs. Philip Baker and Co., Birmingham) represented the defend- ants. Plaintiff alleged that the agreement entered into on June 17th was that he should receive 255. a weekend commission, and a trader's contract ticket between Liverpool and Leeds. The agreement was not sent to him for signature until September 27th but in the meantime he had been travelling for the company and had made inquiries at the request of Mr. J. H. Peterson, who was practically the owner of the concern. He had not receive I either remuneration or the ticket.—ln cross-examination plaintiff admitted that he had not obtained a single order since July Ist, but he attributed that to the fact that he was not supplied with catalogues and lists.— For the defence it was alleged that one condition of the agreement was that the orders sent in must be £260 . per quarter and if plaintiff failed to obtain orders to that extent he must refund to the company a percentage of 5 per cent, on the deficiency. Plaintiff never sent an order and they did not consider there was a binding contract until the agreement was signed. The reason it was not sent to plaintiff earlier for signature was that summer holidays caused a delay.-- His Honour amended the claim t one for services rendered and gave judgment for the plaintiff for witb costs. He also gave judgement for the plaintiff on a counter-claim which defendants had entered against him in regard to a purchase which he made on their

MADAME ELLA RUSSELL.

On Monday November 30th the famous pianist Backhaus and the world-meow-led prima donna Madame Ella Russell will make a welcome re-appear- ance at the Parr Hall, Warrington. Their pr,?sent visit is certainly to be regarded as one of the chief musical events of the season in our town. Moreover it will be their last for _at least two years as Backhaus is making a prolonged tour abroad. It is not only in England that Backhaus has won fame and fortune ; on the Continent he has even greater power. A great tribute to his remark- able Feni us was his selection for the second year in succession, and from amongst the greatest living pianists, to give a lecture course and demonstration entitled, Six weeks with Great Masters "at the Sondershausen Conservatoire. This course Backhaus has just completed with unbounded success and be returns to England for the winter season with his honours thick upon him. Amongst the many engagements of this great virtuoso this season is a tour of 25 concerts with the world's Queen of Song '—Madame Melba. The success of this tour has been phenomenal. Madame Ella Russell has long been acknowledged as one of our greatest singers and she is a prime favourite with concert-goers. In every branch of her art—opera oratoria or the concert platform—she is equally delightful and her appearances are always welcomed. The local arrangements are in the able hands of Mr. R. U. Pearson, and early application for seats should be made at his music warehouse, 75, Bridge-street, Warrington.

Take Woods' Great Peppermint Cure, for Coughs and Colds; it never fails. ls. lid., 2s. 9d.—Advt.

The Examiner Saturday, November 21, 1908.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

mum DROWNING NWSTERY.

At Ditton, on Tuesday, Coroner Brighouse held an inquest on the body of an unknown man, which was found in a pit near Ditton. The man was probably about fifty-four years of age, and was dressed in clothes which bore the stamp of the Prescot Union.— A lad named Dennett spoke to finding the body.—The Coroner stated that the authorities ought to have some system whereby inmates could be easily recognised. He understood that at least half a dozen old men were missing from the workhouse.—The jury returned a verdict of" Found drowned.'

WIDNES MAYOR'S PROCESSION. NUN ' A large concourse of people assembled at the Town Hall, Widnes, on Sunday. to witness the Mayor's pro- cession to Hartland Wesleyan Chapel, Revell-street. At 10.15 a.m the procession started, headed by the Engineers' Band, followed in rotation by the Lancashire Hussars Yeomanry, under Captain Newcombe, Hale Artillery (Captain Rimmer), Engineers, Major Sayce, Bugle Band, , Volunteer Band, Volunteers (Captain Caldwell), the Curporation, police authorities, and prominent members of the town. An eloquent sermon was preached on " Loyalty to God, Obedience to Authority, and Honour to the King," by the Rev. E. G. Charlesworth. He referred to the Mayor's past career and the active part he took in municipal matters, and also the high esteem in which he was held by the public. Reference was made to the excellent work of the ex-Mayor (Alderman S. Owens). A collection was taken, and the amount realised will be handed to the Queen's Nurse's Fund. C ISTNER-KELLNEI ALLI CD. A SATISFACTORY POSIcION.

' The annual meeting of the Castner-Kellner Alkali Company, Limited (Weston Point), was held in London, on Thursday. Mr. G. W. 13 ilfour, the chairman, in moving the adoption of the report, said the financial record of the past year had been very satisfactory. The profit has come within £930 of last year, and as that was only 1 per cent, it might be said to be practically the same. This had occurred in a year of exceptional depression, and the price of coal was very I high ; indeed, the difference in the price of coal more than covered the difference between the two years' profits several times over. They were placing this year for the first time £12,503 to a general reserve accomut. \Last year, as an old Parliamentary hand, he thought it his duty to utter a note of warning that the dividend of 12 per cent, then announced must not be taken as the , standard for subsequent years. This was regarded in some quarters as a pessimistic note, as though Ite meant to say there was some danger in the near future. He saw no such danger then, nor did he now, but in all such industrial concerns as theirs it was well to remember the conditions of trade, that competition was keener than ever, that processes of invention were more rapid and revolutionary, and that processes which were modern to-Jay might become obsolete to-morrow. The , only safeguard against this was financial stability, and during the last three years they had effected a great improvement in this respect. Since the increased prosperity of the company the directors had introduced a system of paying bonuses to the staff. This year they had extended the system to all the workmen who had been in their employ over twelve months. If it worked I well they would make it permanent. Mr. Beilby ' seconded the report, which was adopted. Sir F. E. Roscoe and Mr. Frank Liardcastle were re-elected directors.

1 A DITTON GIRL'S UNPLE ISANr ADVENTURE. A case which aroused great local interest was heard at the Widnes Police Court on Thursday. Rose Jennings, sixteen, a domestic servant, of St. Michael's-road, Ditton, summoned George Hutchinson, a sawyer, of Hale-lane, for assaulting her. Complainant stated that on the Thursday of last week she was walking from her parents' home to her situation, a mile away, along Ditchtleld-road, a long and unlighted thoroughfare. She overtook the defendant, and he opened a conversa- tion with her. He put his arm round her waist and kissed her. She asked him not to do so. Some boys passed by and the man let har go. Afterwards he I attempted to assault her. She scretmed, and he let her go. Afterwards he asked how old she was, and she told him sixteen. She arrived at her destination in an , exhausted state. She took out a summons on Saturday morninl.—The bench de3ided to redan the ease to one of common assault, and fined the defendant £:, with the alternative of a month.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWtON.

NEW CHURCH AT HAYDOCK.

On Saturday afternoon the foundation-stone of the new church of St. Mark was laid at Haydo3k by Lord Newton, who was accompanied by the Lord Bishop of Liverpool, the Rev. P. Ireland (vicar of fravdock), the Rev. T. Stoney (vicar of St. Mark's), the Hon. Hilda Legh, the Rev. Cyril Bardsley (St. Helens), Messrs. Lionel Pilkington, J. Robinson, &c. The church is being built at the Blackbrook end of Haydock, and will accommodate 550. It will be built of stone by Mr. G. Litherlaud, of St. Helens, and the interior dimensions are llift. long by 52 feet wipe. I The pulpit will be of Caen stone, with alabaster I columns, and has been specially subscribed for a.s a memorial to the late Colonel R. Pilkington, C. 8., The population of the parish is 5,300, practically all coalrniners. Lord Newton has given the site and £5OO, the Bishop of Liverpool's fund gives £5OO, and other subscriptions amount to £1,230, while the congregation I of working people have raised £1,540. This gives a total of £3,800 towards the total cost, of £4,500. The architect is Mr. E. H. Lingen Barker. The new church is, practically speaking, a " trial" in the same way as St. Paul's, Bradford (Man.), last year was Manchester's "trial" of a system inaugurated by Sir illiam Houldsworth (chairman of the Bishop of Manchester's Church Extension Fund, and those who act with him) to build thoroughly good churches to the approval of all the authorities, lay and ecclesiastical, at considerably lass cost than heretofore; and in the present case the Haydock people are saving about £l,OOO. The Bishop of Liverpool commented on the splendid effort that was being made in that part of the diocese, and thanked all who had done so much to strengthen the Church and to help that project. Lord Newton laid the foundation-stone, and said that although Haydock was a township of only 9,000 people, yet he was convinced that the new church was absolutely necessary. The work they were doing was indicative of the love of the people for the Church to which the majority of English men and women belonged. He was glad to know there had been during the past three years a great increase in the number of Sunday school children and communicants, and he joined an the hope of their bishop that the blessing of the Almighty might rest upon all connected with the parish.

ST. HELENS.

THE MAYOR AT CHURCH.

• The -Mayor of St. Helens (Councillor A. J. Foote) accompanied by members of the Council. attended service at the Parish Church on Sunday morning. The Rev. Cyril Bardsley, vicar, in the course of his sermon said such problems as unemployment, the necessaries of industry, housing, late hours for shops, &c., were all questions in whicia the Christian ought to be deeply interested. They ought all to try and help in any work that would alleviate suffering, that would conduce to health, comfort, and happiness, to give men a chance of success in life, and whatever might assist to heal the sores and bind up the wounds of the community. That morning they had with them the chief magistrate of the borough, who was one of their own number, and they united In wishing him " God-speacl" during his year of office. SUFFRAGETTE TACTICS.

At St. Helens, on Monday, the adjourned electioneer- ing cases in which John Edivards and Charles Edwards, father and son, of Speakman-road, were charged with damaging the public pavement and electric current boxes by painting election messages thereon, were brought before the Court.—Mr. Haslara Fox said he had seen the Chief Constable and Town Clerk on this matter, and he was prepared to submit to the same penalty as was imposed on other defendants on Friday —viz., 2s. Cd. including costs in one case each.—The Mayor (Councillor Foote) : It is really only to stop the disfiguring on the pavement.—Mr. Turton, deputy town-clerk, agreed to the course suggested.—Both defendants had been summoned on four informations. He would withdraw three cases against both defendants. —The magistrates agreed, and imposed a fine in one case each.

" SECONDHAND " GOODS.

INIPORTANT CASE AT Sr. HELENS. 1`76:74

A somewhat unusTral case was heard at St. Helens on Monday, when Thomas Clarke, marine-store dealer, of High-street, Prescot, was charged with dealing in secondhand goods or articles without a licence.—Mr. Turton, deputy town clerk, said that Clarke's premises in Hamer-street were kept under observation for nine days in October, and a large number of people were seen each day to go into the premises with old jars and and marine stores. When defendant's purchase-book was examined it was found that certain money had been given for the various articles. The question the bench would have to decide was whether the defendant was dealing in secondhand goods or articles. He quoted the definition of " secondhand " from the dictionary.— Detective-inspector Dunn produced the defendant's purchase-book, and said that he had repeatedly told the defendant that he was acting illegally. Mr. Wright I said the same kind of thing bad occurred at Liverpool, and Mr. Clarke had been held to be right. What he had done was not an offence within the meaning of the Act. There was no dictionary in the world that would say that rags and bones could be classed as secondhand goods or articles. Secondhand goods meant things in their original state having been used by someone else, but fit to be used again for the same purpose. About a year ago Clarke was summoned in Liverpool for deal- ing without a licence. The charged was dismissed, but the police appealed, and the decision was upheld. Exactly the same thing had taken place in St. Helens. The bench inflicted a tine of £5 and 17s. costs.

Last year the income of the society was .£183,610, and the expenditure £191,873, showing a deficiency of £3,283. He hoped the time would come when the Congrega- tional Churches .of the country and their own church would set their faces against deficits of all sorts, and so use the money entrusted to them by God, that the work of Jesus Christ would not be hampered and defeated liy anything in the shape of a deficit, but that there would be larger and more generous gifts on the part of those to whom God had given large resources. (Appianse). The CHAIRMAN Said he was present out of respect to their esteemed pastor and also because it was a good thing for members of other churches to associate them- selves with the sister churches to show the unity of tire one Church which was in Christ Jesus. But he was there specially in order that his interest in foreign missions might be revived. He had always taken a deep interest in foreign missions. He was a bit of a missionary himself at one time, as he was in India for 22 months. He saw a great deal of mission work and he knew how much good was being done and under what painful circumstances very often. They were bound to take a vital interest in the great missionary cause, and it showed the zeal and earnestness of a church when it took a deep interest in foreign missions. It was the command of their Lord that they should " Go into all the world and preach the Gospel to every creature," ' and they could not, if they were loyal to Him, refuse to do so. They might not all be able to go out to the missionary fields, but they could contribute as God had blessed them and pray for His blessing to rest upon His Church. They would never realize all the fulness that there was in Christ Jesus until the Gospel was preached to all the nations of the earth. the believed the nations bad something to tell them. They could interpret to them many truths in the Gospel and reveal Christ to them in some aspects in which he was not revealed to them at present, so that there was a great reflex blessing given to them from mission work. All the blessings we in this country prized most highly had come to us from missionaries who visited this land ire years gone by and we ought therefore to do our utmost to bestow on other people the blessings we enjoyed. (Applause). Miss NEWELL then gave a very interesting descrip- tion of the. work in Samoa, of which she is a native. The Samoan group consisted of ten islands, five belong- ing to the German Government and five to America. She worked on one of the American islands, and her father end mother on a German island, and they some- times teased each other by calling themselves Americans lor Germans. Samoa was always hot. There was no ' winter, but they had a rainy season, and when it com- menced to rain it continued for days and days. and they never knew when it would stop. Miss Newell referred to the habits of the people and the splendid work in which she was engaged of educating the young on Christian lines. The natives were very fond of England, and often asked about this country and its inhabitants, and they were deeply indebted to us for making collections once a year in aid of the missionary societies. A good work was being done, and she hoped, therefore, that the interest of the people of England in the missionary cause, would never show signs of abatement. (Applause). The Rev. J. W. CLARK dealt with life in Central Africa and said that in the little church where he taught they usually had a congregation of from 50 to 100 people, and it was their earnest desire to teach the people that God was not the God of the Sabbath only but of every day of the week. The missionaries had to officiate at medical men, and when the natives were dangerously ill had to administer their simple remedies. The weather was intensely hot from 12 to 3 and during those hours it was impossible to do the slightest work. His (the speaker's) greatest joy was to tramp into the adjoining villages to preach the gospel, and while in the forests he had often to wade through a swamp up to his knees or cross a rapidly-flowing river. The speaker appealed for increased financial support for the missionaries so that the work on all the mission stations might be carried on more efficiently and on a larger scale. (Applause.) Votes of thanks were accorded to the speakers.

FREE CHURCH COUNCIL NIEETINCt.—A meeting under the auspices of the United Free Church Council was held in the Wesleyan Chapel on Thursday evening cora- mencing at 7.30. There was a fair attendance. The speakers who addres,sed the meeting were the Rev. John Gunnell and the Rev. J. F. Matthews. The President, Mr. C. E. Parker, being away from home, the chair was occupied by Mr. G. Faulkner. WOMEN'S SUFFRAGE.

PALACE AND HIPP3DROME.

Many people have wondered this week what sort o voice it is that Herbert eithor! has insered for £l,OOO Those who have not been to the Hippodrome are probably still wondering, bat those who have heard the £l,OOO voice understand Herbert Clifton is not a mere vocalist with a good tenor or baritone voice. His "tarn" and his voice were a surprise when the week's performances were opened on Monday evening. Clifton appears on the stage as a rather good-looking street arab, and explains that he has just "sneaked ' into a music hall and heard the turns, which he then proceeds to imitate. His speciality is female vocal mimicry, and the way he sings "Under the Deodar " Lnd "Keep off the grass" in pure soprano tones testifies to the unique quality of his voice. You have only to close your eyes or look the other way, and you feel convinced that a lady with a particularly clear voice is warlolinc!, on the stage. Herbert Clifton's mimicry is also very clever, and his final appearance in a directoire gown and a hat at least four feet across is a triumph. Another big draw in this week's capital programme is the famous Clown Barker with his performing dogs, monkey and donkey. The performance is distinctly amusing, but its chief interest centres in the animals which perform various tricks at a mere nod from their master and evidence the most expert and kindly training. Dave O'Toole, comedian, has the audience in roars of laughter, but his songs and jokes are n.t quite free from the taint of vulgarity. W. H. Downs is an Australian ballad vocalist, and a full blooded negro in the bargain. He sings with plenty of power. A juvenile soprano who made an excellent impression is Edith Cliantler. She has a fine voice and sings with taste. Edtnunda gives a ventriloquial turn which is a bit out of the ordinary, and demonstrateshis skill as a voice-thrower in a convincing fashion. Some very smart hand- balancing feats are performed by Willie and Johnnie, a pair of of youthful coloured performers who bid fair to reach the top of the tree in their line of gymnastics. Pop Dearly appears as a new style comedian and vocalist in a prune coloured frock-suit and tail hat. The cMernatograph pictures are again excellent, and depict a thrilling escape from a harem.

SACRED CONCERT.

The second sacred concert promoted by the Amalgamated Musicians' Union will be held at the Palace to-morrow (Sunday) evening at eight o'clock. A high class programme has been arranged and lovers of music are promised a treat. The artistes will be: The Warrington lisle Voice Choir, Hiss Lucy Davies (soprano), Mr. Tom Boothroyd (solo violin), and the Amalgamated Musicians' Orchestra of thirty performers. NEXT, WEEK'S PROGRAMME. The management announce a novel attraction for next week, the bill being headed by Ford, leginsnia dancer, and Darras, vocalist. These by were originally found performing in the streets of London. They have since played at all the principal variety theatres on the Continent and in the United Kingdom, and at the Palace they will introduce their new act entitled " The boys in white and gold." In c mjunc- tion with this act; the management will run a grand dancing competition in the first house for children under the age of 14 and in the second house for Cam- petitors over the age of 16. The prizes offered are : Children's competition : Ist, ; 2nd, 10s. ; 3rd, ss. Adults' competition: lst,t2 ; 2ud,£l; 3rd,los. The finals will be decided on Friday evening. The other artistes will be :—Lyons trio, pantotnimists and dancers; Cissie Esdaile, comedienne ; Charles Banks, comedian, and his clever dog " Woof " ; Frank Cass, the favourite vocal comedian ; Woodhouse and Wells, the popular comedians and dancers ; Elroy, the arm- less wonder ; Gracie Hunt, comedienne.

The Fair Ground Riot.

The County Police Court at Wrexham was besieged by an eager crowd on Tuesday in order to hear the adjourned proceedings against James Cook, sen., James Cook, jun., John Cook, George Cook, and his wife Betsy Cook, all of Longford-street, Warrington, and Frank Hewitt in connection with the riot which took place at Coedpoeth on the night of the oth inst., and which resulted in fifteen persons being sh The bench ordered Hewitt to be discharged, and remanded the other prisoners for a week, Betsy Cook being liberated on her own recognisances and the others being admitted to bail.
