LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

DEFEAT OF THE GOVERNMENT.

Exciting Division.

The Government has fallen,bnt not without a gallant fight. At the meeting of the Parliamentary Debating Society on Thursday evening, the division on the Right to Work Bill took place and the Ministry was defeated by a solitary vote, amid scenes of great ev.;itement. The Liberals will remain in office for the remainder of the session and then the oppositio3 will be called upon to form a Government. . _

Thursday's debate followed with keen attention and some of the speeches contained passages of more than usual interest. Amusement was not lacking,either. Mr. J. HAWTIIORNE (S. Belfast) led off for the Opposition. lie congratulated the President of the Local Government Board upon h;s honest intentions but said the measure appeared to have been taken up by the Government without thought. It was evidently a one-man measure. The proposals would involve a waste of labour, because the men set to work in the State industries would produce more than they needed and the remainder of their time, which should be employed for the good of the community, would be entirely lost. The speaker indulged in some criticism of the Socialists and had begun to speak of Mr. Blatch- ford and atheism when he was interrupted by Mr. CARR (E. Leeds) who characterised the hon member's statements as "utter rot." The SPEAKER advised the hon. member not to wander from the subject under discussion, and he soon brought his speech to a close.

MI:. W. IL SHERBURN (Secretary for Scotland), said the Government had been charged with always treating , references to tariff reform with levity. The fact was that they so often had that question brought into the , debates that they simply sat there and made a mental calculation as to how many minutes a speaker for the Opposition would be on his feet before he said "tariff reform." (Laughter.) The Liberals were, however, quite ready to meet the arguments for tariff reform— whatever that might be—and no doubt the Tories would I have an early opportunity of introducing their scheme. 1 He believed the real object of the Tories in constantly bringing forward tariff reform was to hide the real !causes of unemployment which were as many as the roots of a cancer and went as deep. Labour-saving machinery had been a cause ot unemployment, because It was used in antagonism to labour instead of to assist it. lie claimed that the Government had dealt with some of the causes of the problem already, and they intended to deal with two others ley nationalizing the railways and taxing land values. (Applause.l There were too many blood suckers on the body politic. Mr. Sherburn supported the Bill in principle, and said the details could be worked out by the committee which would be appointed. Mr. G. OSBORNE (Wellington) admitted that the land monopoly was a great cause of unemployment but con- tended that full advantage was not taken of the present , system. Ile would meet foreiga competition not by tariff reform but by allowing inventive genius to have full sway. The reason it was not encouraged now, was because the management of limited companies was in the hands of the wrong people. He advocated the establishment of patents bureaux in large centres at 1! which workingmen with inventive capacity would be able to submit their plans to experts and have them I placed on the market. Mr. G. HANDS (Barnard Castle), in supporting the Bill from the Socialist standpoint, said he claimed the right to work simply because he was a human being able and willing to work. Mr. J. Mins (Enutsford), said the Bill evidenced an amount of brain work and study, which was mere than could ba said of the criticisms hurled at it. The view that it would interfere with private traders was erroneous. The Tories were always the sturnbliu,g block in the way of progress. The only panacea they could advance was the well-kno am Birmingham brand.( Laugh- ter.) Landlordism generally sapped the life-blood of the people. lie claimed that the Liberals were doing their utmost to improve social conditions. At any rate, they did not retain power for twenty years by specious promises and master )v evasions. (Laughter and applause). Mr. E. OSB3RNE (Newcastle-on-Tyne) sail it wis a matter of no surprise to him that the President of the Local Government Board in introducing the measure should be charged with forcing legislation of a Socialistic character under the cloak of social reform. Almost every measure that was introduced in the House of Commons was either commended or denounced, according to its Socialktio tendencies. Whilst that was very flattering to the Socialists, he did not think it was altogether fair. They did not want to take credit for everything. (Laughter.) He admitted it was possible to get a Socialistic measure from an! individtvalist group, but only when the individuals coin- posing the group had studied economics. If this I scheme were found impracticable when passed, all the ' blame would be thrown upon the Socialists. On the other hand, if it worked successfully it would not be Socialism, but social reform. (Laughter.) The Bill did not introduce a new principle or a real innovation. It was tried eighty years ago and had been triad several 1 times since, and in each case ic had proved a failure, simply because if had been found to be impracticable. It was impossible to separate one group in a civilized country from the rest of th 3 community and expect them to conduct their affairs in a successful manner. Ho contended that the individuals who were confined in the compounds would not be allowed to satisfy their witets and to exercise the privileges of citizenship. Arr. MORTON (Local G3vernment Board) said he did not advocate the colony system and the people engaged iii the State industries would not be separated and componnded. .

Mr. OSBORNE urged tbat he was right in drawing such an inference from the Bill. He opposed the system simply because he believed it would be slavery. A man would not be able to travel by railway, attend theatres, or even a Parliaranntary Debating Society—(laug.hter)— if he only had paper money. How was it proposed to provide places of worship for the various denominations which would be represented in the compounds ? If the Conservative party wanted honestly to raise the ques- tion of religious liberty now was the time. (Laughter and applause.) He contended that the State industries would be bound to interfere with private enterprise. His Socialism did not permit him to confiscate property. iHe did not believe n taxing other people out of existence or forcing them into a corner under the plea of social reform. While he recognised that the President of the Local Government Board bad honestly attempted to solve the problem he firmly believed that the scheme was economically unsound, that it would prove a fiasco, and that its failure would be blamed on Sonialism. (Applause.) zilr. G. IL ROBERTS (Montgomery) opposed the Bill because he considered it was utterly impracticable. Were they going to put the unskilled labourer, the clerk and the artisan together in a bakehouse to bake bread, or in a tailor's shop to make clothing ? The scheme had not received the careful consideration it required and Ile predicted that if it became law it ! would instead of relieving the trouble accentnrate it.As one method of reducing unemployment, he advocated compulsory arbitration to avoid strikes. Mr. NT. MASON (Nevgcastle-under-Lvme) said the , member for Newcastle-on-Tyne seemed to be fearfully troubled by some little " bee in his bonnet " on the subject of religious liberty. He was anxious to know whether in some imaginary compound there would be various churches. Surely ho knew that if there was going to be a paper currency, paper would not be accepted in the collection plate—(laughter)—and if there were no collection there would be no churches and chapels. (Laughter). Having received an assurance from Mr. Morton that the Bill applied to women, gr. isoN said he most , congratulate the President of the Local Government Board that unlike his prototype at Westminster he was attaining to some minimum degree of justice to the other sex. Criticising the Bill, Mr. Mason asked how 1 it wag proposed to provide the right kind of work for ,1 milliners, shop window fitters, tram drivers, bank managers, schoolmasters, or political agents who might be out of employment. (Laughter.) Would the milliner have to work at snow-shifting, or the awe mic conaump- tive clerk at breaking stones ? Perhaps the President of the Local Government Board would say that they would find those people some clerking to do, but they knew that all these soft jobs were given to the sons of Councillors. (Laughter). Mr. NV. L. IV ILLIAME (oswestry) after predicting that we were on the eve of a break down of our com- mercial system and that it would be impossible for hungry and despairing men to continue to walk the streets in which were well-filled shops without some- thing serious happening, advocated tariff reform, a system of insurance against unemployrnent,and methods by which men in search of work would be helped to find it.

Hr. J. F. AwrIZRY (Secretary for Ireland) said it was not the intention of the Government to force those who availed themselves of the State industries into compounds. He regretted the tone of levity which had been imparted into the debate, because they ought to approach the question with the serious determina- tion to unite their intellects in ordr to find a solution. When he heard his friend the Member for E. Man- chester indulging in talk about pints and half-pints of beer—

Mr. R. G. PIERPOINT (E. Manchester) : I take it that when the Secretary for Ireland speaks in this manner he is attacking mc and hists that I was dealing with the qbestion in a jovial, happy-go-lucky sort of! manner. I did nothing of the kind and I maintain what I said before, that the Governmeat 13111 is rubbish. (Opposition applause.) Sir. AWBERY : I was merely reminding the House of an augument he brought forward. Mr. PIRRPOINr : And a very good argument, too. (Laughter). Mr. AWBERT : Subsequently the hon. member was so incensed by what another speaker said that he rose to assure the tionSe that he was genuine in his remarks. (Mr. Pierpoint : " Hear, heir.") I am prepared to credit the hon. member with sincerity, but at the same time he must bear in mind that he is not the only sincere member of the House. Mr. Pianeowr : Mr. Speaker,— The SPEAKER.: Order.

Mr. PIERPOINT: I did not say T was. The SPEAKER again called the interrupter to order. Mr. AWBERY: The youthfulness of the hon. member calls for some indulgence. When he attacks, he must expect to be attacked in return. Continuing, he said it was a notorious fact that any Government that attempted to tackle the unemployed question stood seriously to be shot at. It was a pity that it was not possible for the best intellects in the country to be bronght together to evolve a scheme which would be satisfactory. (Mr. Pierpoint : " Hear, hear.") He did not claim that the Government Bill would cure unem- ployment once and for all ; a succession of measures would be necessary. He was heartily in favour of the principle embodied in it, of establish- ing a Central Authority to deal with the question. At present the municipalities did not arrange their schemes so as to be of the greatest advantage to the unemployed. They were not undertaken at the most suitable times. Mr. Awbery, while admitting that they could not combat the advance which women were making in spheres of employment once considered to belong solely to men, said certain occupations ought to be scheduled as unsuitable for women. Other questions to which the legislature should turn attention were the employment of young children and the tyranny of sweating. They should insist upon the compulsory retirement of all civil servants at the age of sixty years, when they were contribators to a superannuation scheme, and restrict the holding of a number of public appointments by one individual. The State should also endeavour to foster parental interest in the apprentice- ship system. In conclusion Mr. Awbery said the Government had done their best and they thought their proposals ought to be listened to with sympathy on all sides of the House. They wanted to make it impossible for any man or woman to be able to say that his or her poverty was die to lack of employment. (Applause). Mr. J. Mortrox (President of the Local Government Board) having replied to tho various points raised. the Speaker put the amendment that the Bill be read six months hence. The members having shouted their assent or dissent, the Speaker said he believed the " No's " had it. Mr. STINSITRLD (Leader of the Opposition) challenged a division and amid great excitement it was announced that there was a tie, the figures being : For, 35 : against, 35. Those who voted for the amendment included four Liberals, four Nationalists, five Independents, one Socialist and all the Conservatives. The SPEAKER then put the resolution that the Bill be passed and declared that he believed the "No's " had it. Another division was taken with the following result:—For the Bill, 31 ; against, 35. The Govern- ment thus suffered defeat by a majority of one, the Opposition greeting the result with tumultuous cheering. Next Thursday evening the House will discuss aBM for the Nationalization of the Railways, Light Railways and Canals of the United Kingdom.

THE LATE REV. E. J. WILLCOCKS. Memorial Tablet Unveiled. The brass tablet, erected to the memory of the Rev. E.J. Willcocks, M.A., was unveiled on Thursday evening in the Assembly Room of the Boteler Grammar School by Mr. C. S. Burgess (chairman of the Memorial Com- mittee.) Alderman Smethitrst presided Red was sup- ported by the Mevor (Alderman Forshety), Canon Willis, and Mr. J. FL Smethurst. Amongst those present were the Headmaster (Rev. IT. Gray), the Assistant Master, and many scholars past and present. The SECRETARY read letters from several gentlemen, who expressed their regret at not being able to attend. Alderman SMETUCRST said it gave him the greatest pleasure to preside over such a meeting, which was for the purpose of imveiline a tablet erected to the memory of his dear old friend Mr. Wilicocks. He had known him for Some 23 years personelly. The tablet reminded nil those present of the good work he had done in the school. They had only to look round and take their memories back and see the talented young men, who had been ttuned out by the school. Look how many had turned out to be church ministers and teachers of this country ! It must he very gratifying to Mrs. Willocks to know that on the very day of her daughter's marriage, that tablet was being unveiled in commemoration of her dearly beloved husband. Mr. C. S. BURGESS said he wts one of the oldest boys belonging to the school,and he felt it a very great honour to be asked to perform the ceremony, It was the wish of those with whom he was associated that he should unveil the tablet. Hr. Willcoeks was devoted to his duty, and eteldfast in his loyalty to the Governors of school. He had a cheery manner and disposition ; and he devoted himself unselfishly to the prosperity and advancement of the school, lie bore the sufferings of a lone illness like n hero and died as a true Christian , should—in perfect peace with all men. To have given emetically the whole of his lifa to the welfare of the) 'lnstitution cleserged a better recognition than they had 11,1:en able to give hint. If any man deserved well in this town and neighbourhood that man was Edward John Willcocks. (Applause.) His name was revered by hundreds ia many parts of the Empire. The Speaker could enly say that the friendship which began more than 33 years ago when he went there es second master continued through the whole of his life. He would ever look back upon his association with Mr. Willcocks as a bright and happy incident in his own life. He (Mr. Willeocks) implored them to leave the world a little better than they found it. Mr. Burgess then unveiled the tabled which was inscribed in LIU% aal added:—

" Lives of good men all remind us We can make our lives sublime ; And departing leave behind us, Footprints in the sands of time. Let us then be up and doing With a heart for such a fate ; Still achieving, still pursuing Learn to labour and to wait."—(Applause.) The Mayon (Alderman Forshaw) said that he was very pleased indeed to such a splendid tabiet had been erected by the old scholars to their late head- master, !qr. Willcochs. He was sure his memory was worthy of it. He was a good man and a good master. (Armlame).

Canon WILLIS said that in the days gone by, the Rector of Warrington was the Headmaster of the school. He thought it was to the advantage of the school— certainly in his case—that the two offices should have been senarated.(Laighter.)He had a very great regard for Mr. Willcocks. Ile had known him for 20 years out of the 20 years he was headmaster of the school. He never recollected an occasion on which he did not speak to him about the school when he SAW him. Mr. Willcocks miAht have thought that the school was the only thing he had to think about. His great aim was to make it one of the greatest in the country. He always attended the Parish Church, and always delighted in taking the boys. The rev, gentleman was sorry not to see the boys on Ascension Day, but, however, under the new Act there was still a close connection between the Parish Church and the school. (Applause). Mr. A. BROWNE referred to tin time when he was a boy at school. They used to call Mr. Willcocks " Dick," but it was a most endearing term to them. Ho proposed a resolution that the best thanks of the meeting be given to Mr. Burgess for unveiling the tablet.

Mr. E. A. WHITE seconded and the motion was sup- ported by Mr. IZOBRRT PIERPOINT. On the motion of Master J. STRRLI NG (headboy), seconded by Master H. IJAND,.., a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Alderman Smethurst for presiding!. The boys were allowed a holiday in c rnmemoratioa of the event.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE. The Provision—of Meals.

The monthly meeting of the Education- Committee will be held on Monday evening at six o'clock. The School Management Committee recommends that Miss Eleanor J. Forsyth be apnointed Assistant Mistress at the Secondary school and Technical Institflte at a commencing salary at the rate of £llO per annum ; her engagement to commence on the Ist January, 1909, and to be determinable by three months' notice.

===== 0002601_19081121_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
TEIE PROVISION OF MEALS.

On the recommendation of the Sub-Committee appointed to consider the arrangements for putting into force the Education (Provision of Meals) Act, 1931, it was resolved, (a) That further particulars as to the cost of the boilers required be obtained ; (b) that an oven be purchased at a cost of £l9 153.; (c ) that a boiler, cylin ler and tank for the supply of hot water be obtained at a cost of 6s. 6d.; and (it) that authority be given for the purchase of the following, viz. ; —(i) 3 cans for the conveyance of soup, etc., at a cost not exceeding .24 10s. ; (ii) 30 dozen spoons at a cost of £6 10s.; and (iii) 30 dozen plates at a cost of £ll7 s.GcT It WAS also resolved that, whereas some of the children attending public elementary schools within the area of the County Borough are unable, by reason of lack of food, to take full advantage of the education provided for them, and that funds other than pnblio funds are not available to defray the cost of the foo& to be furnished under the Education (Provision of Meals) Act, 1996, application be made to the Board of Education. under section 3 of that Act, for their sanction to the expenditure of a sum (not exceeding £100) out of the rates for the purpose of defraying the cost of the food required. That consideration of the remainder of the arrangements be referred to the Sub-committee, with instructions to report to the committee again thereon.

THE CFIRISTILAS HOLIDAYS.

- Mr. Edwin Plinstoo will move: That &ll the schools in the Borough close for the Christmas holidays on the evening or Wednesday, the 23rd December, 1908, and re-open on Monday, the 11th January, 1909.

SHEPHERDS' DINNER. The annual dinner and meeting of the" Love and Friendship," No. 1,Z51, Lodge of Ancient Sheyherds was held at the Viaduct Hotel on Saturday evening, when about CO members were present. Bro. Davies, P.P.C.S., presided.—Bro. Johnson gave the toast of the , Lodge, and tiro. Pilling responded. They started the year with 4G members. They had made 13 new ones this Tear and 30 juvenile members. This was one of the hest years they had had for 20 yeals. The Lodge began to fall off in 1891, but during the List ten years It had gone up amazingly.—Bro. Barry, P.D.C.S., gave "The Order of Ancient Shepherds," to which the Chairman replied.—A framed emblem of the Order was presented to Bro. R. Harrison for his arduous labours in the interest of the Lodge.

POSTAL AND SAVIIIIIS BANK REGULATIONS

For further particulars see "Post Office Guide," at any Post Odice. POSTA G R STAMPS.

Books containing 12 penny and 23 halfpenny stamps are now on sale at each post office. Price 23.

INLAND ,CORRESPONDENCE.

The term "Inland Correspondence" means cor- respondence posted within the United Kingdom, Including the Isle of Man and the Channel Islands. "Inland Correspondence" is divided into the following classes: (a) Letters (d) Newspapers (b) Postcards (e) Parcels (o) Halfpenny packets Not only letters, but any document or article (with a few trifling exceptions) which can be trans- , mitted by post at all, may be sent by letter post, the only limitation being that of size. LETTER POST. The prepaid rate of postage is as follows : Not exceeding .1 ozs. in weight ...• Id. For every additional 2 0z5.... Id. A letter posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with double postage; a letter insufficiently paid, with double the deficiency. No letter may exceed two feet in length, one foot in width, or one foot in depth. POST-CARDS.

The prepaid rate of pnstage on every post-card is one half-penny, and on every reply post-card one penny. A post-card posted unpaid is chargeable oa delivery with one penny. Cards used as post-cards must be made of ordinary card-board, not thicker than the material used for the thickest official post-card. They may not exceed 5} incites in length by S. inches in width, or be less than 31 inches in length by 21 inches in width. A gummed label (not to exceed 2 inches long and finch wide) bearing the address may now be affixed to the address side of card. If any of the rules relating to a post-card be infringed, the card will be treated as a letter. HALFPENNY PACKET POST.

The Halfpenny Packet Post can be used only for packets not exceeding 2oz. in weight. Printed matter, Bco., not in the nature of a letter, may be transmitted by this method. NYC WSPAPEn POST.

The prepaid rate of postage on a registered news- paper, including any supplement thereto, whether posted singly or with others is id. Newspapers not registered at the General Post Office are treated in the pcst like any other printed matter. - PARCEL POST. The rates of postage on parcels areas follows: For a parcel ... ... not exceeding 1 lb. 0 3 ,y exceeding 1 lb. I, 2 lb. 0 4 i, ff 2 lb. if 3 lb. 0 5 ff f f 3 lb. If , 5 lb. 0 6 ff ff 5 lb. ~ 7 lb. 0 7 „ If 7 lb. o 8 lb. 0 8 ff fl 8 lb. ff 9 lb. 0, 9 If ,' 9 lb. 1! 1.0 lb. 0 10 „ 10 lb. 11 lb. 011 o No parcel may exceed 111 b. in weight. The size allowed for an inland parcel is:— Greatest length... ... ... ... 3 ft. 6 in. Greatest length and girth combined 6 ft. 0 in. For example—A parcel measuring 3ft. 6in. in its longest dimensions as much as 21t. 61n. round its thiekeat part ; a short parcel may be thicker ; thus, if it measure no more than 3ft. in length, it may measure as much as 3ft. round its thickest part. The postage on a parcel must be prepaid. Mark each parcel" Parcel Post." A parcel must not be posted in a letter box, but presented at the counter of a post office, and the sender should see that the weight, size and postage are in order before leaving. _ PARCELS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL).

Parcels aro accepted for parcel post to most foreign countries under conditions similar to the inland regulations, except that for customs require- ments the contents must be declared. The customs charges can be prepaid or collected on delivery. The postage varies to different countries and must • be prepaid. MONEY ORDERS (INLAND.) The postal authorities cannot issuea single money order for more than £4O, and will only cash them at the money order office at which they are made payable, unless they are crossed, when they can be paid through a bank. No fractional part of a Id. can be contnined in a money order. The rates of poundage are :—Not exceeding £l, 2d. ; £3, 3d.; £lO, 4d.; £2O, Gd. ; £3O, Bd. ; £4O, 10d. By paying an additional 2d. and cost of the telegram. of advice, a money order can be telegraphed under certain restrictions. Moser _OtaDEItS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL.)

The maximum amount for which a money order may be issued .for places abroad varies in each particular place. The poundage rates are :—Not exceeding £l,3d. ;£2,6d. ; and above that, 3d. for each additional £2 or portion of it. A fee of 6d. in addition to the ordinary poundage, together with cost of telegram of advice, enables money orders to be telegraphed to certain places abroad. POSTAL ORDERS.

These are obtainable at any money order office in the United Kingdom, and possess the advantage over money orders that they can be cashed at any money order office. If crossed, they can be paid into a bank. They are issued for fixed amounts, rising by pd., from 6d. to £1 Is. The poundage fees are up to 2s. 6d. ; hi. on to 155.; lid. up to 21s. Before parting with a postal order, always fill in the name of the person to whom the amount is to be paid. Each-order is numbered, as is also the counterfoil attached, which latter should be retained and name of payee filled in. The Post- maater-Generel- will entertain no further claim after an order is once paid. Stamps to the amount of sd. can be affixed to make up odd sums.

fELF,GRAMS (BLAND).

The Postmaster-General pOssesses,almost entirely, the privilege of transmitting,cellec,ing and deliver- ing telegrams in the United Kingdom. The charge for a telegram is a minimum amount of Cd., which covers 12 words, and an additional d. for each extra word. This charge includes delivery within the postal district if sent out for delivery from a head telegraph office, but if from a sub-office then within three Miles only, beyond which a charge of 3d. per mile is made front the office door. All charges are, as far as possible, prepared by affixing stamps to telegram form. Address of person receiv- ing a telegram has to he paid for, but sender's name if written on back of form need not be. . Five figures count as one word. The cost of a reply may be prepaid. Telegrams can be repeated on pa3rnent of half the original charge, which extra payment is refunded if error is detected. Telegrams may be posted in wall or pillar boxes, or bands(' to rural postmen, and are wired as soon as received at the nearest telegraph office. Late fees secure attention, in certain cases, to telegrams handed in after the usual hours. The cost of registering an abbrevi- ated address, which must consist of two words, is Li Is. TELEGRAMS (FOREIGN ANT) COLONIAL) Must be Written on special forms and are accepted in the language of any of the principal European countries. The rates vary, and are too numerous to detail here. POST OFFICE SAVINGS BANK. These are established at all money order offices. In ordinary cases the annual deposit must not exceed LtO, and the total amount £2OO. No person can have more than one account. Amounts placed in the bank on behalf of children tinder seven years of age cannot he withdrawn until the child reaches that age, and then only under his or her own signa- ture. The smallest amount received at a time is . Is., and this may take the form of 12 penny stamps ' affixed to it Special form. Each depositor receives a hank book in which the amount of each deposit should he entered at the time of paying in. If the deposit is CI or over. a. special acknowledgment is sent from the head London office. Interest Is allowed at the rate of 2fper cent. Any amount up to and including LI may he withdrawn without previous notice, on presenting a bank hook. 'rho bank book should be _sent each year, postage free,

on the anniversary of the date when„ the accomit was opened, to the Controller of the Savings Bank Department for purposes of checking rind adding interest. The withdrawal of money can bee xpedit4l.4 by telegraphing, the depositor paying cost Of telegrams. P.O. TELEPHONE AND TRUNK LINE CHARGES. The fees for the use of the Trunk lines are as f oliows . The Kangaroos at Warrington.

Brilliant Home Victory.

The event of the football season at Warrington was the visit of the Australian football team on Saturday last. There was rain in the morning, but the day improved and remained fine throughout the match. About 6,000 people assembled, the gate being a good one consider- ing that the lowest charge for admissson was is. Altogether the proceeds realised £lB7, of which the Australians take 70 per cent. Warrington had their strongest team in the field, and the only absentee from; the Kangaroos' ranks was the versatile Messenger, ' whose place on the wing was taken by Bailey. The teams lined out at 3 o'clttck as follows : Warrington—Tilley, back ; Fish, Dickenson, Taylor, and Trehearne, three-quarter backs Brooks and Jenkins, half backs; O'Neill, Dowell, Thomas, A. Boardman, P. Boardman, and Cheaters, forwards. Australia—Bolewski, back; Bailey,Morton,Devereux, ' and Frawley, three-quarter backs ; Deane and Hallo- way, half backs; Abercrombie, ,O'Malley. Burdon, ' Groves, J. Davies, and B. NValsh, forwards. Referee, Mr. R. Robinson, Manningham ' • touch judges, Messrs. W. Jones (Runcorn) and S. Grigson (Salford). Davies started, and the Colonials at once took up the attack, Devereux tricking his opponents well, and making an opening for Bailey, but the Australians' left wing was faulty, and Trehearne ran the ball out to half way. The game was hotly contested at the Warrington end, and twice Morton and Deane came within an ace of scoring, only Tilley's fine defence saving the situation. Eventually Warrington warmed to their work, but Bolewski gave a fine exposition of fell back work. Both Morton and Fish, in turn, failed at penalty shots. The Colonials should most certainly have turned a good opening made by Frawley to advantage, but in quick succession Fish, Brooks, Jenkins, and Neill made runs, , and the Colon'als had indeed a warm time of it. Fish with another shot at goal hit the post. The game was carried on at a hot pace, and eventually Neill made an opening for Brooks, who fell inches short. By the aid of the Colonial front rank, the Australians gained a I footing in the Warrington quarters, but their attack had no sting, and the Wirepullers retaliating, the home backs were seen at their best, and Neil throwing a .‘ide to Dickinson that player made an opening for Fish, I who, although tackled by Abercrombie, threw this player aside, and effecting. a really wonderful recovery, scored' wide out. Brooks failed at goal, and at the interval Warrington led by a try (3 points) to Australia nil. 1 Thomas re-started, and the pace was again of a hot description, the visitors showing poor judgment near 1 the line. Eventually Brooks cleverly eluded the 'Australian half backs, and Taylor made a rare dash. He was stoutly challenged near the line by Davies and Devereux, but despite their attentions he struggled over near the posts' and Fish added the goal. The Australians were not daunted by this reverse, for they played up manfully, and after Fish bad staved off this attack. Holloway broke away, and dodging through the defence he handed on to Courtney, who scored Australia's only try The point gave the Colonial's encouragement, I and they made gigantic efforts to break do s n the War- rington defence. A series of desperate scrums was fought out on the home line, and three times Bailey tried to force his way over but Tilley, Trehearne and Dowell tackled grandly. Brooks finally raised the siege with a run and punt to centre. Here the home front rank I broke away and P. Boardman dribbled over after Thomas had missed a chince at the corner. He was 11 given off-side, however, and the Australians escaped. Fish made an effort on the left and then the ball was sent to Dickenson who cleverly dropped a goal when surrounded by opponents. Final: Warrington, 2 goals 2 tries (10 points) ; Australia, 1 try (3 points).

QUESTIONS FOR TARIFF REFORMERS.

Vl.—lf Protection gives "two jobs to every man," how is it that at the end of March, 1908, one out of every three Trade Unionists in New York State was unemployed.

- The report of the New York State Labour Depart- ment on employment in the first quarter of 1908 shows ' that out of 387,450 Trade Unionists reported upon, as i many as 133,131,0 r 331 per cent., were out of work. It will be seen that the number of men reviewed is , considerable, and large enough to give a result of ' value. Aid that result is, one in three out of work. The report also enables us to trace the record of former years. Here are the official figures, taking us back to 1897 : TRADE UNIONISTS IDLE ON TEE LEST DAY OP mnuen. Number Per cent. 1897 ... 43,654 ... 30-6 1898 ... 37.857 ... 21-0 1899 ... 31.751 ... 18-3 1900 ... 44 336 ... 20-0 1901 ... 42 244 ... 185 1902 ... 36 710 ... 136 1903 ... 41.941 ... 121 1904 ... 103 995 ... 27-2 1905 ..• 54 916 ... 15-1 1906 ..• 37 237 .•. 99 1907 ... 77 270 ... 191. 1908 ... 138 131 ... 351 There have thus been eight very bad years out of twelve. The figures quoted refer to all kinds of unem- ployment, whether arising from lack of work, sickness, accident, labour disputes, or other cause, but lack of work is by far the largest contribu- tory cause. At the end of March, 1908, the unemplcyment from lack of work amounted to ninety per cent of the whole. Nine out of every ten of the 138,131 unemployed were out of work from lack of work. So that at the end of March, about one Trade Unionist in every three in New York State was out of work because there was no work for him to do.

L. G. C. 11.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

Warrington Nursing Association. Sir,—May I urgently ask for a good attendance of sutscribers and sympathizers at the annual meeting on Tuesday evening next at the Town Hall. We have reached a very important stage in the history of this eneficent institution, and to my mind there is orily one of two courses staring us in the face—either we- must increase our annual income or we must reduce our staif.— Yours, ac., ONE OF THE COMMITTEE.

A Call to Duty. Sir,—Last week I wrote under the above heading calling public attention to what I consider to be exceptional poverty through unemployment, and sug- gested that someone in each ward should undertake tn investigate all cases that Call3o to their notice. I further suggested that all those who cared to give any- thing for this purpose should communicate with me so that the distribution could be organised. Since offering my services in this work, and making myself responsible for Whitecross,Ward I have been called away to other duties which will prevent me from carrying out my desires in this direction. How- ever, the work is to go on, and may I ask that all communications be sent to Mr. E. Randles, 76, Cart- wright-street, Warrington, who has willingly agreed to act in ray stead.—Yours truly,

'W. POOH',

10, Bostock-street, Warrington. November 170,1903.

Are Co-operative Dividends Income I Sir,—Will you permit me through your columns to thank those gentlemen of the Council who have been instrumental in raising this question. The matter has no doubt arisen through the agitation that is continu- ally being made to tax the profits of Co-operative Societies in the same way as private companies are taxed. The position is manifestly an unfair one as in the ordinary sense of the term no profits are made.l That which is called " profit "in our balance sheets is simply the difference between cost of goods, plus distribution expenses, and the price these are sold for. i This difference s returned to our members in propor- tion to their purchases and is really a " Discount " and in no sense increases an individual's income. The matter has already been the subject of a Departmental Com- mittee Enquiry whose report was given in June 1905. In one portion of this report the Committee say, "This dividend,is clearly not profit, but merely a return to members of sums which they have paid for their own goods in excess of cost price." We shall be pleased to give any information in our power or to assist any of our members who are in difficulty over this question.—l remain, yours sincerely, J. JARMAN, Secretary, Warrington Co-operative Society. November 11th, 1903.

LOCAL WEDDING. Langdale—Willcocks.

- A pretty wedding which arowsed considerable local interest took place at the Warrington Parish Church, on Thursday, when Mr. Henry Langdale, M.D., Stanley House, Warrington, son of the late Mr. William Langdale, of Rnsholme, was married to Miss Dorothy Annette Willcacks, daughter of Mrs. IVillcocks, Stanley Placer Warrington, and the late Rev. E. J. Willcccks, MA, formerly headmaster of The Boteler Grammar School. The Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) peifortned the ceremony, assisted by the Rev. James Forbes, vicar of St. Cuthbert's,Darwen (brother-in-law of the biicle). The choir was in attendance and Mr. F. M. Darby,Mus. Bac. presided at the organ, the musical portions of the service being' most tastefully rendered. The bride, who was given away by her mother, was charmingly attired in white satin, trimmed with old Limerick lace, and carried a lovely bouquet of roses and lilies of the valley. Her ornament was a brooch of diamonds and ruby, the gift of the bridegroom. The bridesmaids were two children : Nancy Forbes (niece of the bride) and Ourol Samuels (niece of the bridegroom), who wore pretty picture costumes of blue Liberty satin, with white lace caps. Their posies were composed of pink carnations, and they wore pearl brooches, the gifts of the bridegroom. Mr. Percy Samuels of Kntitsford, discharged the duties ofibest man. After the wedding, which was very quiet, there was a family gathering at luncheon in the Masonic Rooms, Bold street. Later the happy couple left for London and the South Coast, the bride's travelling dress being of Peon blue cloth with black hat. They were the recipients of numerous handsome presents.

BILLIARDS.

'TERRIERS" v. RICHMONDS.—The first of a series of billiard matches was held at the Sergeants' Mess, Drill Hall, on Wednesday, between sergeants of the 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment and Messrs. Richmond and Co., Ltd., which resulted in a win for the" Terriers" by no points. Scores : " TERRIERS?' RICHMOND'S. Sergt. Ashton Col.-Sergt. Allardice ... 100 Mr. Taylor . GO Sergt. Fire at the Cable Works.

About one o'clock on Thursday morning many people were aroused from their slumbers by the prolonged sounding of the "buzzer " at the works of the St. Helens Cable Company. The cause of the alarm was the discovery of a fire in the store-room,but bappily h did not assume serious proportions. Information was telephoned to the Warrington Fire Station, and a large contingent of men under the com- mand of Superintendent Turner and Deputy Superin- tendent Turner quickly turned out with both engines. When they arrived the " buzzer" was still giving forth unearthly sounds. A good supply of water was obtained and the flames were got under control in about twenty minutes. The superintendent and a number of the firemen, 13 ith one of the engines, remained on the scene for several hours. The damage, which is comparatively slight, is covered by insurance

"I don't mind telling you," continued Mr. Crosfield, " that if ever my ship comes in—(laughter)—and I can afford to do a little bit on my own, I have at the back of my mind a little project of a pretty brown trout stream where those silvery finny darters abound, in that bonny land of Scotland—. in fact, a small stream on the west coast there, and a turn at the sea trout when they are running after a spate in July, COMPS about as near my ideal of human happiness as anything I know of. Having pointed out that to-day the designation "sport" embraced games,Mr. Crosfield sald he knew a very good friend,a sportsman,wbo said, "Well,give me the worst of sport before the best of games." (Laughter.) He (Mr. Crosfield) heartily disagreed with him. He supposed there were some fishermen present that night— (hear, hear)—and if they happened to have gone through the experience of fishing for about a week with an average of one rise a day they would no doubt endorse his view that his friend's statement was pretty wide of the mark. Dewsbury v. Hunslet. Bradford v. Wakefield. Halifax v. Treherbert. Merthyr v. Huddersfield. York v. Hull. Widnes v. St. Helens. Mid-Rhondda v. Ebbw Vale. Aberdare v. Barry.

Hockey.

Latchford's match with Sale Moor at Sale last week resulted in an easy win for the visitors by 6-1. Up to the interval the game was even, but afterwards the home team backs could not cope with the Latchford forwards, the latter displaying very good combination. The goals were scored by Scott (3), Banks, Spensley, and Parker, one each.

Mr. Wilkinson's team of " Old Boys" beat the Second Team at Latchford 3-2 in an enjoyable game, and it was evident that these so-called " Old Boy s" are far from dead yet !

To-day the First Team entertain Westbrook. The following players are selected :—W. Unsworth, goal ; C. Plinston, T. Dixon, backs ; D. Travis, J. B. Wilkin- son, J. • Albirtson, halves ; F. Newell, II Parker, 11. Scott, E. Banks, W. Soensley, forwards. Referee, Mr. W. B. Cobb. Bully off, 2.4; p.m.

The Seoond Eleven play Stockport "A" at Heaton Moor and their team is :—J. Appleton, goal; J. Wilkin- son, W. H. Pickles, backs ; A. Newton, A. Knight, F. Tole, halves : A. Starkey, L. Kenworthy, H. lboth, W. H. Atinards, C. Hackney, forwards. Referee, Mr. Harry Warburton. Train leaves Central Station at 1.30 p.m.

CURRANT AND BANANA COONERY. HIGHLY SUCCESSFUL EXHIBITION IN LONDON.

His Excellency M. A. Romans, the Gree't Minister to England last week opened the great National Currant and Banana Cookery Exhibition •at the Holborn Town Hall, London. This Exhibition of Cookery, which was by far the most successful ever held, excited such keen interest throughout the Kingdom that over 25,000 exhibitors entered, and the exhibits numbered con- siderably over 100,000. Among them were many dis- tinct novelties in the art of cookery, and some 200 varieties of recipes were submitted. Competitors from all parties of the country were present, and in all Some 40,000 people witnessed the Exhibition. At the close the exhibits were distributed among various charities, including the Ragged School Union, the Salvation Army, the Church Army, Dr. Barnardo's Homes, and the Browning Settlement.

A novel wager was made in Cape Town last week. One sportsman backed himself to run around Table Mountain, shouting as he ran, "Take Woods' Great Pepperment Cure for coughs and colds," if the other sport would sit on the Pinacle of a Mos9ue and sing "Rule Britannia." The wager is to be decided shortly and is looked forward to with keen interest.—Advt.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE. " The Great White Chief."

The patrons of the Royal Court Theatre are provided with something novel and original in the dramatic line this week, the attraction being Lingford Carson's "The Great White Chief," which is presented by the : author's company. There is a healthy tone about the drama and while exciting situations abound, an abundance of comic relief is also forthcoming, and further variety is added by the inclusion of several vocal numbers. The "Great White Chief " is the head of a tribe of Indians who make their home on the South American prairie, but, desiring to see some- thing of the world, ho comes to Europe and is promptly taken in hand by society. He spoil makes enemies who do their utmost to compass his downfall, but in vain. The hollowness of the life is suddenly brought home to him and he yearns to breathe the free air and follow the healthy pursuits of the prairie Shaking himself free from his persecutors, he lases no time la returning home and he unfolds to the astonished natives a far from alluring picture of our ways in this quarter of the globe. The business by which so many per.„,),le thrive is, to his mind, only a form of "thieving" and —to the natives this was the most startling revelation they actually build work- houses in which to keep those whom they have robbed." Needless , to say, after this little narrative, the Indians are convinced that theirs is the happier lot. Interwoven with the plot is a pretty love story and, notwithstanding all that villainy can devise, it ends in the approved style, much to the satisfaction of the audience. Mr. Mansell Fane achieves conspicuous success in the title part for which his fine, manly bearing eminently fits him. He possesses no mean vocal talent and his songs have proved highly popular. The author (Mr. Lingford Carson) impersonates Reindeer, the dumb chief, whose splendid fidelity is the brightest feature of the story, and creates a great impression. Mr. Carson presents a brilliant study of the part and well deserves the enthusiastic appreciation which has been unmistak- ably manifested each evening. Mr. George Butler as Hartley Dean, the unscrupulous villain, and Miss Dulcie Lawrence as Heather Stafford, his dupe, have unenviable parts but they play them exceedingly well. Miss Pollie Denville is a pleasing Latika, infusing much sympathy into her portrayal. Great fun is created by the adventures of George Rupert Walker (Mr. Leonard Maitland) whose weakness for "ginger beer" involves him in many scrapes, and his master- ful partner Martha Sane Cnrtley (Miss Amy Ross Clyde). All the minor parts are in capaVe hands.' The mounting of the piece is exceptionally elaborate, bgtb the prairie Woes and the Scottish banquetting room being triumphs hi the way of scenic effect. Mr. Joseph Beecham, the new proprietor, and his manager (Mr. A. M. Loader) may be relied upon to run the theatre on enterprising lines, and there are many good things in store for local plav-goers,inelud. ing a visit from the Moody-Manners brand Opera Company and the famous " Merry Widow." The: orchestra is to be augmented and various parts of the theatre re decorated, and the management will do , their utmost to act on any suggestions which may be forthcoming for the increased comfort of patrons. A new rule is that children in arms are not to be admitted to the theatre. Mr. Loader, the manager, has had a valuable experience of theatrical work. Before going to St. Helens he held appointments in London and at the Shakespeare Theatre, Liverpo and his management of the Warrington theatre bids fair to be attended with great success. Mr. S. Smiley I is the as.istant manager. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

Next week we ate to have a return visit from Mr. Carlton Wallace's company of players in the beautiful love romance entitled "The Love that women desire." Mr. Carlton Wallace is well known as the author of "The Village Blacksmith," "The Lancashire Lad" and other dramas, and "The Love that women desire" has proved one of his most popular and successful productions. A strong company has been engaged, including Mr. Sydney Leyton as Philip Carnaby, and Miss Amee Grattan Clyndes as Sybil Thorburn. There TV ill be magnificent special scenery by Francis N. Bull.

WARRINGTON SHOPBREAKER. Prisoner Sent for Trial on Four Charges.

Frederick Hallows, joiner, Cumberland-street, was again brought before the Warrington magistrates on Thursday oh the charge of breaking and entering the lock-up shop of Messrs. G. and W. Collins, wholesale grocers, of Sankey-street, some time between 9.20 p.m. on Friday, November sth, and 12.15 a.m. on Saturday, November 7th, with intent to commit a felony. He was further charged with breaking and entering the warehouse of Robert Henshall, wholesale provision dealer, the Old Vicaraee, Knutsford-road, some time between 6 p.m. on Monday, June the Ist, and 6 a.m. on Tuesday, June 2nd, and stealing therefrom a cash bar, containing £49 19s. 3d.. the money of Robert Henshall, with breaking and entering the lock-up shop of Thomas Daintith, gunsmith &co Bridge-street, some time between 10 p.m. on Friday, November Gth, and 9 a.m. on Saturday, November 7th, and stealing there- from a pocket knife and a pair of scissors valued at 7s. Gd. the property of the said Thomas Daintith ' • also with breaking and entering the shop of Harry Mather, butcher. 75, Church-street, on October 27th and stealing therefrom 295. in cash, also 511bs. of beef, 231bs. of mutton, a pair of gloves and a tOwel, valued at 365. the property of the said Harry Mather, the prisoner pleaded guilty to all the charges. With reference to the first charge P.C. Green stated that on Friday, the 6th November, he examined the premises of Messrs. Collins at 11.30 p.m. and found them apparently safe. He noticed a spyholo in the window and a light burning in the shop. At 12.15 he again examined the shop and observed a man crouching against the safe near the shop window, and he had no doubt it was the prisoner. He was not attempting to break into the safe. Having tle key of the yard gate witness went in, and on entering the yard he found a piece of wood had been ‘ut out of the back door of the shop. He tried to get through the hole and found it was too small. He blew his whistle and got the block of buildings surrounded by officers and other men, and forced the door. Whilst forcing the door he heard a noise as if someone was pulling slates off the roof. On getting in the premises he hurried up to the top Storey and found a hole in the roof. He got through tht, hole and searched the roof, but did not find anyone. He stopped till about 6.30 and then left, and he knew the premises were watched. When the prisoner was brought to the police station he recognised him as the man he had seen in the shop. He was at the police station on the 17th November when the tools produced were shown to the prisontr. Prisoner admitted that the tools were his, and also showed how he carried them. He told then that he got into the yard by getting over the wall at the rear of the premises. P.C. Woodland said that on Friday the 6th of November he was on plain clothes duty. He was in Sankey-street about 12.45 and he heard a police whistle blowing. When he arrived on the scone he found the Chief Constable,a number of police officers and civilians , surrounding the premises. He took part in the proceed- ing and afterwards he searched the shop and found a number of tools at the end of the counter near to the safe, consisting of a brace, a bit, a wood chisel, a screw driver, a table knife, and a pair of scissors. He at once took possession of them and prisoner subsequently admitted they were his. P.C. Fish said he was on plain clothes duty in Winraarleigh-street, and was engaged with other officers watching the premises. About 7 o'clock in the morn- ing he saw the prisoner coming down the spout, from the roof of the Guardian "office. He ran about 380 yards before he was captured by two workmen in Museum-street. Prisoner (to witness) : You would not have caught me in a month. " James Cook, manager of Messrs. Collins, and liviag in Hood-lane, Sankey, gave evidence as to the security of the premises when he left them. With reference to the second charge, Joseph Leigh, clerk to Robert Henshall, Knntsford-road, said when. Ihe left the effice at about five minutes to five on Mon- day, June Ist, there was a bag of money consisting of ,1£33 101. in g01d,.£16 9s. in silver and Odin copper,under a number of papers in an unlocked drawer. The door of the race would not lock on account of the mis- &thy, of the frame. Next morning he found a cash , box had been removed from its former position and left in the middle of the room and the cover of the typewriter had been removed. John Wilcocks employed at Robert Henshall's said he left the shop about six o'clock. He fastened the door by a bolt at the top of the door and there was a hole in the door for ventilation purposes and through this hole the bolt of the door could be reached and drawn back. He arrived at the premises about five o'clock the next morning and noticed that the bolt had been removed. Harry Birch, manager at Messrs. Henshall, Limited, said that it was his practice to take the money home with him, but on the night in question he told Leigh to hide it under slot of paper. Dealing with the next charge William Daintith said he was employed by his brother at his shop in Bridge- street. On the Bth of November he and his brother loft the premises secure. On returning the next morn- ing he found the back_ door had been tampered with and a large piece of wood had been cut out. He found the dealt forced and papers strewn all over the floor. The till which contained nothing had also been broken open. He also found that the powder safe had been forced. At the time he did not miss the knife and scissors, but he recognised them as his property. With reference to the fourth charge, Harry Mather said he loft the shop secure at 8.30 p.m. on the 28th of October. On arriving at the shop the next morning he found the back door open and a piece taken out of it. He examined his premises and found the money and goods in question were missing. Mr. Birch, manager of Messrs. Henshall was recalled and said prisoner was formerly employed by the firm but had been discharged. Sergeant Williamson stated that on the 18th of November he charged prisoner, who in answer to the first charge said "Yes, that's right." To the second he , said " Yes, that's right. I am very sorry about it. I would like to make it up with Mr. Henshall after- wards. I got the money. It was in a small bag. I threw the bag away in the river." Prisoner admitted the other charges. The Colonials never really got their passing into operation after the first quarter of an hour, during which period Warrington were clearly second-best and bad some lucky escapes. As the hot:nesters got warmed to the fray they took the upper hand in all departments, and Brooks, Dickenson and Taylor completely paralysed the attack of Dean, Devereaux and Morton. There are not two stronger spoiling centres playing than the Warrington pair, and the AuAralian insides found themselves overpowered whenever they got the ball. Warrington handled in mach more effective style, and with a dry ball would probably have put up a larger score. Fish was too good for Frawley, and the way he beat the defence and held off such a robust tackler as Abercrombie, was a treat to witness. His try was such a one as only he could have scored. Even Messenger is nct the try-getter that the Warrington crack is.

The opening stages of the game suggested that Warrington ciere going tO be well-beaten, but they kept their heads until the Australian "fire " had been worked off. It was only during this stage that the burly Colonial scrummages had any advantage. After- wards the Warrington six played a splendid game in which P. Boarduaan, Dowell, Chester and Thomas were always prominent. O'Neill was good, though less robust, and he is credited with the pass which led up to Fish scoring. Personally I thought the home backs got possession from a fly-kick which went into Brooks 's hands, but I might have been mistaken. All 1 the Warrington men did grandly, Tilley being as solid as a rock in the rear, and even Treharne held his own among strange company. The best men on the Colonial side were O'Malley and Davies among the forwards, and Bolewski and Devereaux in the back division. I think some credit is due to the halves also, for they strove desperately to open out the game, though they were overplayed by the Warring- ton pair. Bailey, the substitute for Messenger, ran very: strongly and speedily but was lacking in finish at tunes.

A keener game has not been seen at Warrington for some time, and the Colonials certainly showed every inclination to rough it. The tackling all round , was most determined, and a regular duel was witnessed between the outside halves Brooks and Dean. The Warrington man won and increased his reputation, though he also came under the ban of the referee for one tackle of his opponent which was too vigorous. There was some rather wild kicking in forward rushes, and Jenkins and O'Neil (Warrington) and Davies (Australia) were among the sufferers. A. gate of £lBl was satisfactory considering the wet morning and the high prices.

The match at Everton on Wednesday was a brilliant I one in every respect, and fully upheld the reputation of the Northern Union for providing interesting focit- ball. A victory by the " Kangaroos," though it was by the narrowest possible margin, was rather a surprise, but they deserved their success. ,It cannot be said that the better team won, however, for the combination of the League thirteen was superior, and they lost solely became they were a man short for more than half the game. Holroyd, the Yorkshire half, had a rib broken. and thereafter Brooks worked the scrum in fine style and made some grand openings for the men behind. Leythara on three occasions got clear away, only to try his punt and follow-up trick on being faced by the lull-back. It failed every time, and the Warrington folk present groaned at the thought of the absent Fish. Evan some of the Association enthusiasts wanted to know why Leythana didn't beat the full back.

The scoring was very even. AUS tralia secured the first try, and alternate successes by each side brought the score to Australia 8, England 6 at half-time. The homesters made great efforts on resuming and took the lead at three=quarter time when Leytham slipped over at the corner. Then the C donials relied on Messenger. He took shots at goal from inside and outside the h all-way line, and at the fourth attempt he sent the ball sailing over the bar from mid-field, and won the match. He is par excelleive as a goal- kicker, though even he missed an easy one right under the pots. Leytharn's goal-kicking was" off," or the result would have been dlffment. The gate realiscd £285.

NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the Town Connell of the County Borough of Warrington have resolved that each of the above streets shall be levelled, Paved, fl egged, channelled, and made good. in pursuance of Section 6 of the above Act, and have further resolved. " That the specifica- tions, plans and sections, estimates, and provisional appor- tionments, prepared by the Borough Surveyor, In respect of each such street, be approved." - The said specifications, plans and sections, estimates and provisional apportionments, or duly specified copies thereof will be deposited during one month from the sixteenth day of November, 1908, at the offices of the Borough Surveyor at the Town Hall, and will be open to Inspection at all reason- able times. BEAUTIFUL WARRINGTON. Another Letter to the Children. Ai? II • _,, , • • My dear Beautiful Warrington Children, I hear that )ou think it time you had another letter. Farmers, Farm Labourers, Vig,nerons, What shall I tell you about ? Well, as most of your Orchardists, Market Gardeners, and time is spent in school, perhaps you would like to hear about the smallest school I was ever in. Off the coast Domestic Servants, are offered of France there are about 250 small islands, most of them only large rocks. They are called the Islands cf ASSISTED PASSACES Chausey, and only one is inhabited. One day while to staying in Normandy I visited this island. After two hours sailing from the coast of France we reached our destination. The steamer cannot touch the island WESTERN AUSTRALIA because of the rocks all around, so natives come out from the island, and it was with a little fear, that I from jumped from the side of the steamer into their boat. Soon I was landed on the island. I had beard before I 4'5 to Z 7. 101 s. visited this place, that I should find the people living as the Bretons did in this land 800 years ago. Free Grants of 160 Acres. On our way up from the beach, I passed some huts, 50,000,000 ACRES AVAILABLE FOR SETTLEMENT. built of stone, with thatched roofs, looking very much ; like a roughly-made pig-sty, except that they had a ! Excellent Climate. small chimney. Later on, I asked to be shown the Work can be carried on all the year round. houses of the 70 inhabitants. " You passed them ' soming up from the beach," I was told. NIVEr for ore For Illustrated Pamphlets and further particulars moment had I thought, that those pig-sty-looking apply to the Agent-General for Western Australia, buildings were the homes of the people. I had peeped /5, Victoria Street, London, S.W. into one deserted-looking place, and its earthero floor, open fire-place and rough walls led me to think, that it belonged to a people of centuries ago ; but I was told that the homes of the present generation were very little different.. Lately, on enterprising company Las built an hotel upon the island, which is open in the summer months. Perhaps now es the natives are brought more into contact with refined people, they will become more civilized in their home-life. I found much to interest me lon the island. The natives are not left quite to them- selves for a good priest has been living on the island for the last 17 years and is not only their religious instructor but he is also the schoolmaster, doctor, in fact he is a friend in need at all times to these poor people. They have now a tiny church and the smallest school I ever saw. I entered it, and it contained two rough tables and some benches and ono scholar, a boy who was learning French catechism. Be was so dirty and his book was as dirty as himself, and I'm afraid the schoolroom matched him ; but evidently he Was fond of learning. A French gentleman who was with me, ques- tioned him and he replied very intelligently, repeating page after page of the catechism without one mistake. I was rather amused to see hanging from the ceiling of the room strings of onions and bunches of herbs. Evidently, the good priest used the schoolroom as a larder also. The priest's house stands near to the school, and very pleased we were to meet this goer' man. He welcomed us warmly and invited us into his garden. Children, you have no idea what a struggle he has to keep anything in that little garden. The soil is very poor, and the rough Atlantic storms would have destroyed it again and again, if he hal not built a high wall round :it. In the middle of the garden stood a very p-imitive sun-dial, having the pointer made from a piece of old Lin. The priest's garden was the only one I saw on the island. This gcod man loved Mother Earth's gifts and when he presented me with a few flowerr front his tiny garden I felt they were too precious to be allowed to fade and on my return to the hotel I carefully pressed them. I have pasted them in my Album and I never look at those flowers without thinking of this noble priest, living a lonely life in order to teach these poor people about God. In our wandering over the island we came across two little boys who were too well- dressed to be natives. They had a saucer spoon and were carefully getting something out of the rocky pools along the shore. After watching them I exclaimed suddenly' ''Why they are getting salt." One little fellow l i lobed up and said n very good English " Yes, we are getting salt." Finding that this little French boy spoke English, T entered con- versation with him. Ile NH me Le was staying on the island for two months, and as there were no rhops, this was their only war of procuring salt, T could tell you more of my visit to this ialand. hut now I must write a little about bulbs, rs I have been asked about their culture, and from results which I sow at the last Bulb show, I know m!stakes were made. One little girl kept her plant in the dark uutil the day of the show. We all want our members to have a pot of hulbs. Tt is not too late to plant them. For ' potting bulbs any good garden soil it ill do, but it is better to use a compost of two narts of rotten sods or loam, one part of decayed leaves, with sun:lent sand to make it porous. Pot the bulbs firmly in the soil, covering until only the crown appears, leaving an inch space on the top for watering purposes. After potting give it good watering to settle the soil and RS soon as the surface is sualciertly dry. place the pots in a cool airy place, at the same time keeping dark. When the shoots are ahont 3 or 4 inches high, bripg them gradually to the light, finally placing close to a sunny window. From this time they should be watered freely.—Yours affectionately,
